- Truth Firewall (2pts): after each DONE claim it runs acceptance checks on the final repository state; four outcomes (VERIFIED_DONE, REJECT_DONE, HUMAN_REQUIRED, ERROR), 3 worker attempts, a SHA-256 digest of the task and checks recorded at activation so a later edit returns HUMAN_REQUIRED. Checks are only `file_exists`, `python_function` signature, and black-box JSON cases. The README admits the digest does not stop deliberate rewriting of both contract and digest, and gives no accuracy figures.
- Squadra (1pt): `prove` runs the agreed command on frozen files and keeps the native result ("Proof bound to the exact code"); `deliver` sends a reviewer an immutable packet; `check` confirms proof, review and candidate still match; reviewer must not be the author; `probe` records an independent test with a negative control that must fail; commands are argv arrays not shell strings; evidence is content-addressed and the event log is hash-chained in SQLite. Its own timings come from about 9,980 of its events, not an independent benchmark.
- Untyped (2pts): a 155-line TLA+ model of the harness-to-tool protocol on a durable runtime, with recorded runs as JSONL checked against it. Safety invariants: AtMostOnce (each effect at most once), ApprovalBeforeEffect, BudgetHeld. Verdicts accepted/violated/rejected map to TLC exit codes. Seeded bugs it catches: a retry with a fresh idempotency key after a lost ack runs the tool twice ("the refund goes out twice"), approval-proceeds-on-timeout, budget counted per attempt. A vacuity config proves the invariants are not trivially satisfied.
- Runtape (2pts): reruns one recorded model call with parts of the context removed, measures the base rate first, uses a one-sided Fisher exact test with multiple-comparison correction, and `runtape fix --write-test` emits a regression test that re-runs the bad decision 10 times against the live model. Self-reported, tiny: 10/10 vs 0/10 on an inbox example; 2 stars, unreplicated.
- Failure drills (1pt): 26 scripted drills (D01 to D26), no model and no network, each makes one mistake in a throwaway git repo and a guard catches it; includes deleted test, skipped test, hollow assertion, "false done", phantom package, diff budget, and a "guard audit" drill that reads a guard log.
- agent-trials (1pt): runs the same tasks through installed agent CLIs; the verifier's exit code is the verdict, `verifyPaths` are restored from the repo after the agent runs, and scripts the verify command names are fingerprinted, so the agent cannot pass by editing its checker. Its demo (3/3 for three models, costs $2.50, $1.55, $1.81) is labelled a smoke test.
- SWE-Sweep (5pts; Meta, Stanford, Harvard, UW, SWE-bench authors): 100 repos, 22 languages, about 4k real bugs, no hint of bug type or location. Best setup fixes under 5% (Sol 5.6 xhigh 4.7%, $7,230; Opus 5 xhigh 1.3%, $5,363; Luna 5.6 high 1.4%, $28). Grading (README): a patch scores 0 if any previously passing visible test fails; otherwise the fraction of subtasks whose hidden tests pass; micro-averaged. Precision on non-buggy code is not measured beyond the regression check.
- Self-bench (4pts): builds Harbor tasks from a repo's merged PRs: instruction from the PR, an authoring agent writes hidden tests and a reference solution, and a task is accepted only if it fails with no solution, passes with the original, passes again on rerun, and survives an independent review. Reports accuracy and cost per task with a Pareto frontier; the README states no task counts or contamination handling.
**Comments (unverified):** `maxnyriev` c49926177 on SWE-Sweep: the prompt says bugs exist, so models are eager to please; how many "fixes" touch working code, and report precision next to recall. `martianvoid` c49864672 on trace tampering (see 2.6).
**Links followed:** all eight repos above; github.com/facebookresearch/swe-sweep's paper link (swesweep.com/paper) not opened.
**→ Lain:** The tools converge on three rules Lain should hold as graders, and each maps to an existing seam. (1) Bind the proof to the digest it ran against: Squadra and Truth Firewall both hash the contract; Lain already has a content-addressed Store, so `Grader::Verified`'s refutation (`lib/lain/grader/verified.rb`, verified) could record the tree digest it verified and `check` that digest at land time; "proof is void if the tree changed" is a one-line invariant. (2) agent-trials' checker restoration is the exact fixture-sealing mechanism the ROADMAP proposes (line 1487): restore the grader's own files from outside the agent's reach after the run, and fingerprint any script the verify command names. Add as a spec on `Grader::TestHarness`. (3) Untyped's AtMostOnce is a property Lain should pin on `Effect` retry: a lost reply that triggers a retry with a new key doubles a write; Lain's Effect interpreter is where an idempotency key would live, and the invariant is checkable on the journaled Effect log without a model. Item 61/PM-style tests: Untyped's vacuity config is a good habit for any invariant spec ("also show the bad state is reachable"). Runtape's "measure the base rate before blaming a cause" is a free precondition for any counterfactual-context experiment on `DryReplay`, but remember item 42: replay is not a scorer for a model swap, and Runtape reruns the live model for exactly that reason. SWE-Sweep's maxnyriev point is the false-positive-on-working-code rate Lain's two-pass `Grader::Verified` is built to reduce, and a ready bench task for it.
**→ ROADMAP:** "Behavioral & verification graders" bullet (line 281, verified) and the fixture-sealing item (line 1487). New candidate C6.

### 2.9 Auditing the benchmarks and evals themselves: Do Agent Benchmarks Do What They Say?, AnyRank, Rare Failure Eval, Tessary — ids 50043658 (1pt), 50092115 (1pt), 49961283 (1pt), 50033189 (1pt)
**Article:** `2609.37315` (abstract opened): treats each tool's advertised interface as an executable contract and audits 34 mutating tools across four benchmarks, confirming 7 tool defects and 1 evaluator property. At least 5 of AgentDojo's 25 mutating tools diverge from their advertised surface; on tau2-bench the evaluator rewards refuelling a suspended line (on 1,120 constructed paths). Notable case: a clinical benchmark's tool reports each write executed under a no-write design the interface does not disclose, and the grader treats that message as evidence, so its action success rate "measures whether the request carried the expected payload, not whether any record changed". The abstract's own checker numbers: 0 false positives in 25 flags, but it flagged 2 of 5 negative controls and missed most injected defects (29 of 33 misses had a covering clause and no probe). `2610.14603` AnyRank (abstract opened; a robotics paper): sequential testing that allocates trials to hard-to-separate policies, valid ranking at any stopping time, up to 50% fewer hardware evaluations. Rare Failure Eval (opened): a synthetic CPU-only simulation, 1,024 worlds, showing selection accuracy and decision cost rank evaluators differently when failures are rare; variance allocation raises the wrong-pick rate 44.4% to 52.6% in the concentrated-risk setting yet cuts utility lost by 27.8%; with 4 draws a 0.001-probability failure is seen 0.4% of the time. Tessary (1pt, README not opened; the story text claims) cheap classifiers over every trace, then agent triage on flagged traces, "5x cheaper than sampled evaluation at 1M traces"; that figure is the vendor's own and unverified.
**Comments:** none on these four.
**Links followed:** the two arXiv abstracts and the Rare Failure Eval repo.
**→ Lain:** Two directly usable results. (a) The tool-contract audit is the reverse of what Lain's tools face: Lain's tools are real, but its bench uses `Provider::Mock` and `Effect::Handler::Mock` (CLAUDE.md, verified), and any mock that reports "executed" for a write is the same defect; a spec that every Mock write handler either changes state or declares no-write in its result schema keeps the Mocks honest. (b) AnyRank and the Rare Failure Eval together say: when sweeping arms, spend trials where arms are hard to separate (sequential allocation with an anytime stopping rule) rather than a fixed n, and report regret (cost of a wrong pick), not only selection accuracy. That belongs with item 57's "print the MDE before a sweep runs": add a stop rule so a sweep stops when the pair is separated.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends items 41 and 57 (prior file §12); sequential allocation is a new sub-bullet under "M3c ... swept axes". No separate item.

### 2.10 Deterministic inference: precision divergence and Vosti — ids 49825325 (1pt), 50012359 (1pt)
**Article:** `2609.26621` (abstract opened; TMLR 2026): greedy decoding in BF16 vs FP16 on identical hardware diverges on 49% to 100% of prompts (six models, 1.1B to 7B, plus 12B), because a one-token flip cascades; the driver is the top-two logit margin at the LM head; selective FP32 recomputation of the head when the margin is below a threshold lifts exact agreement by 22 to 36 points on A10G (12 to 21 on L4 and A100) at under 4% latency, only at batch size <= 4, and it vanishes at batch >= 8 and under end-to-end FP8. `2609.38981` Vosti (abstract opened): formalises "same prompt and initial sampler state gives bitwise-identical logits", reports that vLLM's batch-invariant mode and SGLang's deterministic mode produce different logits under some execution variations, and verifies an engine with Verus plus a Triton kernel analyzer; the abstract has no performance numbers.
**Comments:** none.
**Links followed:** the two abstracts only.
**→ Lain:** Corroborates the "~9% temperature-0 flip" noise floor in item 41 with the mechanism, and tells Lain what to journal beside `seed`/`temperature` on a local arm: the numeric precision of the served weights and the batch size (`OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL`, item 59(f)). Since the top-two margin predicts divergence, a cheap diagnostic exists: where an Ollama response exposes logprobs, record the top-two margin per turn and report the share of turns under a threshold as "how reproducible is this run likely to be". It also bounds `Bench::Variance`'s determinism axis (`lib/lain/bench/variance.rb`, verified): that axis proves the harness renders deterministically, and these papers are why it still cannot promise the model does.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 59 (local-arm discipline) and 41; no new item.

### 2.11 I deleted 168,000 lines of comments; my AI agent didn't notice — id=50090484 (35pts, 17c)
**Article:** openstation.blog (opened). Same project with and without about 168,000 lines of comments (almost a third of source); Claude Code, Opus 5.5 high effort; two tasks (tile all windows; keep a window on top, which needs persistence across reloads), 3 runs per branch per task; lint/types/tests plus a hidden test on the bigger task; a separate Claude session scored with a pre-written rubric on randomly named branches. Averages (article's numbers): small task score 18.3/19 with comments vs 19.0 without, tokens 2.18M vs 1.71M (-22%), cost $1.42 vs $1.04; bigger task 21.7/23 vs 22.0, tokens 9.06M vs 9.12M, cost $3.84 vs $3.95, with single runs ranging about 7M to 10.6M. All runs passed all checks; all six runs on the bigger task caught the persistence trap. The author's caveats: one model, two tasks, n=3, AI judge, author's own codebase; "rules out a large effect but not a small one".
**Comments (unverified):** `huijzer` c50091960: the comments were AI-generated, so the reader shares the writer's context; test human-written comments. `phil-martin` c50091978 lists dose-response arms (half removed, 10% wrong, 10% very wrong). `skeledrew` c50092987/c50093091: effect would show in tool-call counts and rediscovery tokens, not correctness. `kingstnap` c50093374: shows examples of agent-written comments that are phase references that go stale. `stuaxo` c50093380: "waypoint comments". `sinn357` c50099631: a "why" comment stopped a regression from recurring. `mehdi_zare` c50107255: with no test covering comments, deleting them looks like a win. `bbor` c50092015: the hidden-trap task may be too common in training data to discriminate.
**Links followed:** the article.
**→ Lain:** CLAUDE.md's comment policy (WHY only, density set by `lib/lain/timeline.rb`, ticket references banned) is a taste rule backed by `bin/comment-census`; this is the first measured test of the premise "comments change agent output". Result: no correctness effect at n=3, a possible token effect that is within the 7M to 10.6M run spread. Lain can do the experiment properly and cheaply because it owns the harness: a `Workspace`/file-view arm that strips comments from what `read_file` returns (a pure transform at render time, so the repo is untouched), with tokens, tool-call count and grade recorded, n large enough to resolve the spread, and the dose-response arms `phil-martin` lists. It also tests `skeledrew`'s specific prediction (fewer useless tool calls with comments). Run it at a variance-aware n: the article's own run-to-run range is wider than its effect.
**→ ROADMAP:** none needed beyond a Context-axis row; candidate C7 (low cost). Stays within "The organizing idea" table (line 37).

### 2.12 Two small harness-behaviour measurements: "Do not guess" and failed-write retries — ids 49868753 (97pts, 41c), 50068634 (1pt)
**Article:** earnanhonestdollar.com/bench (opened): a web-extraction benchmark of 42 page pairs over 7 page types, one run per contestant; across 16 models the made-up field rate fell from 70.7% (405 of 573) to 20.2% (116 of 574) with the instruction "Do not guess" plus null for missing values. Firecrawl still made up 24 of 36 missing fields with the instruction; a plain fetch plus GPT-6 Luna made up 5 of 36 for $0.0049 for the whole run. As a checker, Luna caught 38 of 49 made-up values with 0 false rejections of 47 correct ones; Jev caught 23 of 49. Limits stated: synthetic pages, one run each, free tiers. The title's 71% to 20% is for this extraction task only. agent-write-path-runs (opened): 500 runs (5 model routes x 10 failure cases x 10 repetitions) on mock tools with injected faults (lost replies, rate limits, 5xx, malformed replies, wrong IDs), neutral prompt, German user prompts. Correct end state per 100: DeepSeek Flash 91, Qwen3.8 27B 89, Qwen3 14B local 70 (task success only 30; false success 10), Claude Haiku 4.5 60 (task success 20, duplicate writes 10, false success 20), Ornith 1.5 35B 49 (duplicates 38). Two models duplicated an invoice in 10 of 10 runs after the invoice call timed out; two of five gave up after one call on 429/503 every time. n=100 per route so roughly +/-6 to 10 points; one prompt, mock tools.
**Comments (unverified):** `ben_w` c49875238 and `datsci_est_2015` c49869455: prompt incantations decay as harnesses absorb them; `astrange` c49884213: "move controls out of the prompt and into an external system, but ... it costs more"; `nizarmah` c49871093: if it can be measured it can be checked programmatically.
**Links followed:** both repos' pages.
**→ Lain:** The write-path run gives a ready-made fault set for Lain's Effect layer: lost reply after a write, retry on 429/5xx, malformed reply, wrong ID. They are fault injections on `Effect::Handler::Mock`, graded by end state rather than the model's final message, and the false-success column is exactly "final message says done while state disagrees" (item 43's rule, read from the Effect log). "Do not guess" is a Prompt-slots arm (ROADMAP line 49, verified) with an objective measure (fabricated-field rate against a page with known absences), and a good test of whether such an instruction survives a context strategy such as compaction.
**→ ROADMAP:** C6 below (fault injection arm); the prompt arm is a row under "Prompt slots".

---

## 3. Context, instruction files, skills, MCP and token tools  (SCOPE: context-and-code-mode)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** The prior run's watch item is resolved: CliffCompaction is a real paper (arXiv 2609.26779) with an MIT, PyPI-published, scaffold-agnostic API proxy (github.com/nguyenvuthientrang/cliffcompaction), and its mechanism is the opposite of summarisation: only truncate or drop, never rephrase, and never compact a compaction (re-derive from the original every time the threshold is crossed). CORVUS (2607.22711) is the second context mechanism with numbers: a synchronised file registry instead of stale read snapshots (9 to 50% fewer input tokens, up to 37% fewer cycles). Skill routing now has a scaling measurement (2610.18522: recall 73.6% to 27.4% with 1,000 distractors, explicit preselection holds 73.9%). Claude Code's silent AGENTS.md skip (a remote flag, not a bug in the loader) is a clean example of ambient configuration that changes the Request without any record. Anthropic's Opus 5.5 guide adds four harness-level levers Lain can sweep (time signals, turn-scoped reminders, per-message effort, end_turn continuation). Around them is a wave of 1 to 5 point token-trimming Show HNs; only three (Context Diet, squeeze, D-Engine) publish a with/without task-success measurement, and none measures the bill including turns, which is what item 57 requires.

### 3.1 Claude Code reads AGENTS.md only when telemetry is on [fixed] — id=49814947 (486pts, 284c)
**Article:** The author (blog.szypowi.cz, fetched) found Claude Code 2.1.277 advertised AGENTS.md support but loaded it through a built-in plugin `agents-md` whose availability check queries a remote flag `tengu_agents_md_mod` with fallback `false`. With `DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1` or `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC=1` the flag never resolves and a local AGENTS.md is silently skipped, no warning. Detection method: a directory holding only an AGENTS.md with a canary word, `claude -p` asked for it, two sessions per configuration (the first only fetches the flag), plus a string search of the binary. Workaround: a one-line CLAUDE.md containing `@AGENTS.md` (imports do not depend on the flag). Numbers: none beyond version strings; the evidence is the author's own tests (issue 95690), not independently verified.
**Comments:** `mpoteat` c49815363 (unverified, claims to be a Claude Code engineer): a "rollout artifact", the flag exists as a remote kill switch, fixed in v2.1.281. `mpoteat` c49815421: AGENTS.md support was built as a "mod", a plugin with a new "function hook", launching soon; mod source at github.com/anthropics/claude-code/tree/main/mods/agents-md. `rickette` c49815527 and `locknitpicker` c49815683: the only difference between the two files is the filename, versus "a common-ground format". `crooked-v` c49820994: Claude Code steers the model to arbitrary Bash instead of harness tools, which bypasses hooks and permissions (issues 90450, 89251). `user43928` c49879390 (in 49874728): Anthropic guidance says instruction files under 200 lines.
**Links followed:** blog post (above); Context Diet (3.6) and guardrails-plugin (4.5) both depend on the same new "mods" hook API (v2.1.287+), which is a real extension surface to compare with `Middleware`.
**→ Lain:** Not a Lain defect, but a spec-shaped lesson. Lain's session header should list every instruction source that reached the Request (item 52 already asks); this thread shows why: the source set depended on a network flag, and the only symptom was model behaviour. A seam spec with a canary word in each instruction file, asserting it appears in the rendered `Request`, is the cheap mechanical form (the author's own method). Also supports Lain's no-remote-flag stance: rendering is a pure function of `(Timeline, Toolset, Workspace)` (CLAUDE.md, verified).
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 52 (record every instruction source in the session header); add the canary-word render spec to it. No new item.

### 3.2 You said no MCP (Pi adopts MCP and "codemode") — id=49906637 (687pts, 363c)
**Article:** earendil.com (fetched). Pi moves MCP from extension (`pi-mcp-adapter`) into core. "Codemode" is a JavaScript sandbox that runs on the harness side (not in the tool environment), orchestrates MCP calls, keeps its state in the session transcript rather than the file system, can ship small builds as WASM, and loads automatically when MCP is configured. Pi's tool loadout can now mark tools deferred or codemode-only; earlier MCP extensions lacked the metadata for that. The example: 167 open Linear issues classified with 4 workers, calls of 255 to 756 ms, 331 earlier calls in the session. Argument: MCP's weakness is composability and the fix belongs partly in servers (structured data, discoverable docs).
**Comments:** `rcarmo` c49907375 (unverified): codemode is overkill for a narrow scoped agent but right for a coding agent; "better MCP design saves everyone a lot of trouble"; chaining notes at github.com/rcarmo/umcp/blob/main/docs/CHAINING.md (c49907383). `sunaookami` c49907131 and c49908276: disabled subagents and nearly every tool but Bash/Edit/Write/WebSearch, replaced the 10k+ system prompt, sessions of 150 to 300k tokens with no compaction, subagents lose the shared cache. `cyanydeez` c49907641 and c49909907: runs github.com/Tarquinen/opencode-dynamic-context-pruning (DCP) locally, adds an eviction command and a ratio so context stays at 64 to 128k; "waypoint" instead of summary; a summary sits atop the cache stack so only the top is rebuilt; day 3 of one session with 3M tokens removed (anecdote, no numbers). `statenjason` c49907956: `mcporter` exposes MCP servers as shell commands so output pipes through `jq`. `CharlieDigital` c49907780 and `AznHisoka` c49907985: MCP server count is still rising (bloomberry.com/data/mcp). `evantahler` c49909278 (arcade.dev): "the model never needs to see the file" (file handles instead of bytes). `hobofan` c49906957: an MCP SEP for uploads.
**Links followed:** DCP repo (fetched): non-destructive (history never modified, placeholders swapped in per request), range-mode compress tool the model calls, nested compression, dedup of identical calls, purge of errored inputs after 4 turns, protected tools and patterns. States the cache trade-off in prose and publishes no measurement (4.4k stars). It is the same shape as Lain's `Context::DedupeToolCalls`, `PurgeFailedInputs`, `Prune` and `ProtectedPatterns` (all verified in `lib/lain/context/`), which Lain already treats as swappable combinators.
**→ Lain:** (a) Code mode: Pi's design choice that the interpreter lives harness-side and its state is transcript, not disk, is the property Lain's Timeline wants (state is replayable and forkable). Compare with Lain's M5 code-mode item. (b) DCP is external corroboration that dedup, error-purge and protected spans are the community's converged set; the open question it leaves is cache cost, which Lain can measure and DCP does not. (c) The "eviction to a ratio, then compress, then evict" loop (`cyanydeez`) is a stateful two-threshold policy; as a `Context` combinator it needs a Timeline-derived state, not a mutable one, to stay pure.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 48 (context sweep with a budget axis): add a "waypoint eviction" arm and report cache-read tokens beside it. Under `### M5: orchestration, memory, code mode`, no new item.

### 3.3 Anthropic's Opus 5.5 prompting guide and "Getting the most out of Opus 5.5" — ids=49874728 (207pts, 227c), 49946567 (252pts, 162c)
**Article:** The guide (platform.claude.com, fetched) and the blog (claude.dev, fetched). Harness-relevant, with the numbers the guide gives: (1) default effort is now `medium`; medium matches or beats Opus 5 at high; changing the top-level `effort` between requests invalidates the prompt cache, while a per-message effort change (beta) keeps it. (2) Unattended runs: a text-only `end_turn` can be a progress report, so treat it as a report, keep a checklist, send a short continuation naming open items, and cap automatic continuations at 2 or 3. A system-prompt paragraph names four early-stop shapes to avoid; it must be present from the first request or it invalidates earlier thinking blocks. (3) Progress updates arrive as `thinking` blocks, empty at default `display`; a harness reminder after about 5 silent tool steps, appended as a turn-scoped system message (`clear_at: next_user_message`), "roughly halved the share of tasks with a long silent stretch, with no measurable change in cost" (Anthropic's testing, no n given). (4) Time signals for multiagent harnesses: append `elapsed 340s / 1200s` to each message; teams given a budget kept quality while finishing "considerably sooner"; the budget is advisory. (5) Pasted text: wrap in `<pasted_content id="ab12">` with a per-request random id and tell the system prompt; "tags are plain text and can be imitated". (6) Remove "think carefully" lines; `reasoning_extraction` refusals when prompts ask the model to reproduce its reasoning. The blog adds: keep the task list in a file (`TASKS.md`) because it survives compaction, and fan-out to one subagent per service then verify evidence.
**Comments:** `yfontana` c49947973 (unverified): Opus 5.5 cache reads are priced at 5% of input, not 10%. `GaryBluto` c49950642: the built-in classifier poisoned a session and switching to a weaker model did not help. `gregwebs` c49878254: review by an independent-context model finds important issues on non-trivial changes. `ffsm8` c49878059: hitting weekly limits sooner on the same workload.
**Links followed:** none beyond the docs.
**→ Lain:** Four of these are sweepable `Context`/`Agent` arms with a clean control: (a) time-signal line `elapsed/budget` as a tail reminder, in fan-out teams (extends item 53); (b) the 5-silent-steps reminder as a turn-scoped message, cost-neutral claim checkable against `cache_read_input_tokens`; (c) end_turn-with-open-items continuation policy, capped at 3, as a loop arm, graded on completion not turns; (d) random-id delimiter on pasted text versus bare text, scored on obedience-to-planted-instruction beside task score (extends item 45). The 5% cache-read price changes the break-even in every cache-thrash estimate (item 56): journal the price used with the run.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends items 45, 53 and 56; propose one new item (see candidate 4 below).

### 3.4 CliffCompaction (two submissions of the same paper) — ids=49822774 (1pt, 0c), 49874916 (1pt, 0c)
**Article:** arXiv 2609.26779 (abs page fetched; the export API returned 429 so the ID is title/date-confirmed from the abs page, not API-vetted). Repo and PyPI package exist: `github.com/nguyenvuthientrang/cliffcompaction`, `cliffcompaction` (MIT, Python 3.11+, command `cliff`). Mechanism (repo and a listing, fetched): a proxy between the agent and the provider (Anthropic Messages, OpenAI Chat Completions, OpenAI Responses). When the estimated token count crosses a threshold (default 200,000; README says use 100,000 to 128,000 on 200 to 250k windows) it rewrites the request: system prompt, task and the last 3 assistant-step turns verbatim; tool calls reduced to one-line signatures; tool results kept only if 500 characters or fewer, otherwise dropped on the argument that the file can be re-read; assistant text and thinking kept (optionally capped); images dropped; previous summary discarded on re-compaction ("we never compact a compaction"). Compacted history is keyed by the original prefix's hash chain, so later requests get the stored version substituted. Claims (abstract; self-reported): up to 50% lower cost under bounded context, over 10 points gained on Terminal-Bench within less than two full-context runs of cost, Kimi K2.6 at 73.27% on SWE-bench Verified with a 32K threshold versus 73.87% at full context, KernelBench speedups 2.23x at 200 steps and 3.58x at 400. The repo says nothing about provider prompt caching and warns that a scaffold's own compaction breaks the prefix matching it relies on. The September Dettmers post claimed "100M-token sessions"; the paper's claims are the bounded-context ones above.
**Comments:** none.
**Links followed:** repo README and the ai-tldr.dev tool page (above).
**→ Lain:** This is the strongest new arm for the context sweep and it is a natural `Context` combinator, because it is a deterministic function of the original content: a Lain Timeline is lossless, so "never compact a compaction" is free (re-render from the Timeline each time) and purity is preserved, where the proxy has to key by prefix hash. Concretely: `Context::Prune`/`Compact` (verified) are the neighbours; add a `TruncateOnly` arm with parameters `threshold`, `keep_recent`, `tool_result_cap` (the three CliffCompaction knobs) and compare against model-written `Compact` with the same budget. The one unmeasured thing in the paper's public material is cache: each threshold crossing rewrites the whole prefix once, so the experiment must report cache-read tokens and billed cost, not only success, with the 5% cache-read price (3.3) journalled. A second hypothesis it makes testable: dropping tool results over 500 characters is safe only when the tool is re-runnable; Lain's `Effect` records know which tools are pure reads.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 48 (five-tier context sweep with a budget axis): add tier T5 "truncate-only, recompute from original" and the three knobs; also item 57 (score on the bill). Closes the prior run's CliffCompaction watch item.

### 3.5 CORVUS: Context Optimization and Reduction via Underlying Synchronization — id=49883080 (2pts, 0c)
**Article:** arXiv 2607.22711 (abs page fetched; ID not API-vetted, 429). Conventional agents append every file read and its result chronologically; snapshots go stale when files change, causing errors and re-reads. CORVUS decouples file-read actions from their observations: a synchronised registry of relevant files, and each reasoning cycle injects only the files' current contents. On SWE-PolyBench Verified and SWE-Bench Pro with 4 LLMs: 9 to 50% fewer average input tokens per task, 15 to 32% shorter final prompts, up to 37% fewer reasoning cycles, pass rates "comparable". No repo on the abs page.
**Comments:** none.
**Links followed:** abs page only.
**→ Lain:** Lain has the right shape twice over: `Workspace` is sent, not stored, and `Context::DedupeToolCalls` already drops stale duplicate reads as a pure projection (verified). CORVUS is the stronger version: replace every `read_file` result with a pointer and render current contents of the live set at the tail. That is cache-hostile at the tail only, and it makes the Timeline provenance question explicit (the transcript no longer shows what the model saw, the Request does). Arm: `FileRegistry` combinator over `DryReplay`-renderable requests, scored on stale-read errors (a read followed by an edit followed by a use of the old text). Supports item 49 (state-only render).
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 49 (a state-only render arm) with a second concrete state-only design; cite 2607.22711.

### 3.6 Token-trimming tools: Context Diet, squeeze, ctxfw, compaction.dev, Indexio, D-Engine, others — ids=50024608, 50076734, 49893845, 49845110, 49978602, 49827661, 49911910, 49902441, 50047627, 49955121, 49925593, 49831138, 50083619, 49782439
**Article:** Mechanisms and evidence, from each repo or page (all fetched except UsageGuru, griz, sgrep, ambits):
- **Context Diet** (50024608, github.com/drkokorev/context-diet): a Claude Code "mod" (the new JS hook API) that rewrites Bash/Grep/Glob/JSON-MCP results longer than 8,000 characters into a digest (failures with location, counts of passes, JSON skeleton, repeat-run shows only the diff) and saves the full text to `.context-diet/out-NNN.txt`. Evidence, self-described as "not a benchmark": 7 Q&A tasks, 2 Sonnet agents each, 7/7 both arms, 8 tool calls versus 13; a 600-test bug-fix task on Haiku/Sonnet/Opus, 18 runs, both bugs fixed in every run, 6 calls versus 8, tokens down about 7% including about 50k fixed instructions per agent. Two findings worth keeping: Claude Code already moves very large outputs to a file, so the gain is fewer steps, not tokens; and on a failing command Claude Code keeps about 10k characters and drops the tail, where failures usually are (opt-in capture mode rewrites the command, which can stop permission rules like `Bash(pytest:*)` matching).
- **squeeze** (50076734, cauebraz/squeeze): stdin pipe `tool | squeeze`, format-aware (terraform, pytest, jest, kubectl, SARIF and others), states what it cut, `--raw-dir` keeps the original. Claims "fixes the same defects with 65 percent fewer tokens" across 4 agent tasks (self-reported, details in `bench/README.md`); error and warning lines are never dropped by contract; 95% coverage gate, golden files, fuzzing. No release.
- **ctxfw** (heuristicolab/ctxfw; 3 submissions): AST stubbing of context to depth levels D0 to D3. Its benchmark page measures only tokens and parse validity: zulip `users.py` 328,539 to 83,913 (-74.5%), posthog `team.py` 1,333,525 to 681,273 (-48.9%), airflow `dag.py` 411,010 to 127,882 (-68.9%), with `len(text)//4` as the token count and no task success. It claims every repo resolves "under 20 ms" while its own PostHog P95 is 250 ms. The headline "67%" has no agent-success arm.
- **compaction.dev** (49827661): local proxy/gateway; examples only: Codex input 8,388,356 to 7,212,095 (-14%, measured), output estimates marked "est."; claims 48 to 50% on uncached sessions and 5 to 10% on cached ones. No quality benchmark.
- **spenmcke/compress** (49911910, 49902441): proxy plus a fine-tuned Qwen that removes tool-result text before it returns to the model, "leaving kv cache untouched"; 29.6% fewer tokens measured from `response.usage`; `curl | sh` installer; no success-rate comparison.
- **Indexio** (50047627): Rust MCP server indexing the codebase; claims 50 to 60% fewer tokens versus built-in tools, near-instant lookups, weaker on symbols (it tells you to use an LSP for those); "aggressive tool call logic which attempts to override built-in lookup tools".
- **D-Engine** (49925593): model returns SEARCH/REPLACE blocks, applied in a shadow git worktree, `tsc --noEmit` is the only gate. 10 frozen tasks, one attempt each: quality 48/50 versus 48/50 for an agentic loop, tokens about 2,100 versus about 93,000, 0 versus 1 broken commits. Small n, author-reported.
- **griz** (49831138, 1pt, tweet only), **sgrep** (50083619), **ambits** (49782439), **UsageGuru** (49955121): launch pages with no measurement I could find.
**Comments:** `SerhiiDrozdov` c49933651 (49911910): token count is not the metric, check success rate; critical info is often 1 or 2 lines (HTTP status, failing test, stack trace, exit code, path), so preserve such anchors exactly. `stdl1b` c49912110: how was 30% measured (answer: `response.usage` through the proxy). `cudder` c50047714 (Indexio): a language-server plugin already cut tokens; answer `iohotspot` c50049260: LSP is better for symbols, this covers the other 90%.
**Links followed:** all repos above.
**→ Lain:** `Tool::Bounds` (verified, `lib/lain/tool/bounds.rb`) takes the opposite stance to Context Diet: "whole artifacts refuse", a command's over-ceiling output returns nothing of the payload and names a narrower action; enumerations disclose with an in-band notice. Context Diet's digest-with-spill (counts, every failure with location, full text on disk, repeat runs show a diff) is a third arm on the same question, and its report that failures sit at the dropped tail is a direct test for Lain's refusal: does the model's "narrow it" retry cost more turns than a digest? This is the output-shape axis item 50 flagged (result shape as a second axis). Arm set: `{refuse+narrow, head-truncate, digest+spill, spill-only}` on test/build logs, scored on turns and bill (item 57). The anchor-preservation point (`SerhiiDrozdov`) is a cheap grader: seed a log with one decisive line and assert it survives each arm. Everything in this list is a per-tool or proxy trimmer; per item 57's precedent (RTK: 89% saved while cost rose 17%), none of the percentages above should be trusted until the bill and turn count are in the same table.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 57 (score token-saving arms on the bill, count turns, assert the tool was used) and item 50 (result shape axis); new bench task suggestion in candidate 5.

### 3.7 Context-tax, Viberuler, Context-autopsy: measuring what loads before you type — ids=50117929 (1pt), 50009232 (5pts), 50093737 (1pt)
**Article:** **context-tax** (gethrbr/context-tax, fetched): reconstructs per-turn pre-prompt cost from the `usage` figures on each API reply (the exact total) and estimates rows by chars/4. Its sample (stated as "fabricated for illustration") has about 40,000 tokens per turn: Claude Code's own tool schemas 22,000 (55%), system prompt 3,200, MCP about 2,600, skills 2,000 (31 skills, 9 listed as bare names because the listing hit a cap of about 1% of the window in characters), agents 2,400, instruction files 1,600, hooks 400, unattributed 4,800 (12%). It actually starts each configured MCP server and runs `tools/list`. **viberuler** (master5d/viberuler, fetched): `audit` over local Claude Code/Codex transcripts: cache-hit rate, "context amplification" (main thread, example 1088x), subagent spawn cost (cold context 50.2K tokens, 33.1K re-paid on each of 3,234 spawns), "ghost tokens" (oversized results over 4KB were 54% of admitted tokens; dedup of repeat reads worth 2%), and dead MCP servers (zero calls in 10,700+ sessions; removing them cut median cold context from 49.9K to 41.1K). Author's own rig, illustrative. **context-autopsy** (50093737): "very quickly vibe coded", not fetched beyond the title.
**Comments:** none.
**Links followed:** both repos.
**→ Lain:** Direct validation of item 40 (initial-context bytes as a `Compare::Table` column; `Bench::DryReplay` renders the first request for free). Two ideas to take: report the share of the first request that is tool schema (55% in the sample) as its own column, and flag a configured-but-never-called tool/server as "dead weight" per run, which is the bench-native form of item 57's "assert the tool was used". Viberuler's 2% for dedup versus 54% for oversized results is a useful prior: trimming big results beats deduplication.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 40 (initial-context bytes column): add "schema share" and "unused-tool count".

### 3.8 CAVEWOMAN, Terse, and "What's Next": terse output, and turn endings as choices — ids=50036322 (3pts), 49995809 (25pts, 18c), 49804006 (2pts), 49813512 (1pt)
**Article:** **CAVEWOMAN** (arXiv 2606.24083, abs page fetched, not API-vetted): 8 models, 5 datasets, 5 reduction levels. Output compression cut realised cost about 1.4x to 2.4x per API model (up to 3x best case); input compression raised net cost about 1.15x on the five-benchmark mean (up to 1.8x worst dataset, 2.7x under stronger compression) because models answered longer, and accuracy dropped; for non-reasoning models about half the generations were correct yet their compressed text no longer entailed the model's own unconstrained answer. Two-channel protocol: accuracy, realised per-item cost, agreement with the unconstrained reference. **Terse** (lowenbjer/claude-terse, fetched): output style plus `SubagentStart` and `UserPromptSubmit` hooks (a 576-character reminder, about 145 input tokens per turn, because the style is diluted in long sessions). On 12 public prompts with Opus 5.5: chat words 5,959 to 1,793 (-70%), median reply 636 to 181 words, output tokens -45%; 20 prompts on a real codebase: -46% words and -51% cost on Fable 5.1, -54% words and -32% cost on Opus 5.5. Judge: an Opus 5 model counting style violations (11.1 to 1.1 per 1,000 words); one pass at high effort. Limits it states: cost falls less than words (context dominates), and Opus 5 subagents kept about 13 em dashes per 1,000 words. **What's Next** (kvitapp/kvit-plugins, blog only): ends a turn with a short report plus 2 to 4 choices.
**Comments:** `TZubiri` c50004127 and c50014010 (unverified): terse output means less chain of thought, hence less compute and more errors. `lowenbjer` c50011034: no accuracy loss noticed. `preetam960` c50005281 / `lowenbjer` c50010967: system prompt instructions are forgotten after a few turns, so a reminder is re-injected every turn.
**Links followed:** the two papers' abs pages and Terse repo.
**→ Lain:** CAVEWOMAN supplies the missing control for Terse's claim: its split says output compression is the cheap lever and input compression is not, which lines up with Lain compacting inputs (Compact) and shaping outputs (a Workspace/system arm) as separate axes. Terse's own finding (instruction decays over a long session, so re-inject per turn) is item 51's reminder-cadence axis with a number attached (145 tokens a turn). Terse reports no task-success arm and a single judge pass; the CAVEWOMAN three-channel score is the grader to adopt.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 51 (instruction framing and reminder cadence); adds "output-style arm scored with accuracy, cost and agreement with the unconstrained answer" to the style sweep.

### 3.9 Skills: routing collapse, trace compilation, correction-derived skills — ids=50083980 (1pt), 49850532 (1pt), 50069498 (3pts, 1c), 50035864 (1pt)
**Article:** **Lost in Implicit Routing** (arXiv 2610.18522, abs fetched, not API-vetted): SkillsBench tasks and golden skills fixed, irrelevant skills added. With 1,000 irrelevant skills loaded-skill recall fell from 73.6% to 27.4%, the zero-load rate rose from 9.3% to 51.6%, and explicit selection from the same library kept recall at 73.9%. Masking only the descriptions of irrelevant skills largely restores loading (semantic interference); asking the agent to plan skill use explicitly still gives lower recall than preselection. **TraceCompiler** (2608.02680, fetched, submitted 3 Aug 2026): clusters agent traces and compiles deterministic workflows; an edge is admitted only on unique value attribution (15,775 def-use edges: precision 0.928, recall 0.943 versus 0.711 F1 for adjacency); 34 observed Venmo calls reduced to 11, 15 of 21 leave-one-out passes; the AppWorld 0.993 precision is acknowledged as partly circular. **shadowclone** (theonly1me/shadowclone, evals folder fetched): turns user corrections in on-disk sessions into skills; its eval is the careful part: 24 synthetic tasks in 8 preference families, 2 dev and 1 held-out case each, 5 setups (agent alone, existing skills, plus routing, plus handwritten, plus learned), 8 held-out cases kept private with only hashes public, decoy tool results that must not leak into learned text, disposable repos with isolated agent home, 95% intervals that resample only three repetition groups ("coarse"). Results were in a separate `evals.md` I could not read. **eval-skills** (confident-ai, 50035864): a documented build-and-descend loop, 19 commits, no benchmark.
**Comments:** `shadowclone` c50070437 only asks about opencode support.
**Links followed:** the three above.
**→ Lain:** The routing-collapse paper is the scaling axis item 50 and item 52 lack: the skill/tool "disclosure sweep" is currently over arms; this adds the number of distractors (0, 10, 100, 1,000) and a "descriptions masked" control that isolates interference. TraceCompiler is a bench-side idea, not a Lain runtime arm: Lain's Journal of Effects is exactly the input it needs, and "admit an edge only on unique value attribution" is a checkable rule over `Effect` records. shadowclone's held-out-hash design is worth copying for any Lain skill-learning experiment (item 62: treat every optimiser's grader as an input).
**→ ROADMAP:** extends items 50 and 52 (add distractor-count axis and masked-description control, cite 2610.18522); item 62 (held-out hashes).

### 3.10 Instruction-file auditors: Ruleherd and RuleReceipt — ids=50083765 (1pt), 49911193 (3pts, 1c)
**Article:** **RuleReceipt** (rulereceipt/rulereceipt, fetched): extracts rules from CLAUDE.md/AGENTS.md/Cursor/Copilot files with an English instruction-word heuristic (not an LLM), reads the newest transcript, routes each rule to the narrowest check (structured checks on git/file operations are the only source of a confident FAIL; literal checks; judgment rules UNCLEAR unless `--llm`). Numbers: over 559 public rules files 63.2% of rule lines are not instructions; a PreToolUse guard blocking on command literals refused 62.8% of 16,336 replayed tool calls, 2.5% after narrowing, still with false blocks; the Stop hook blocked 2 of 13 real sessions. It sees only what the transcript records. **Ruleherd** (princejain756/ruleherd, fetched): keyword-pair contradiction tables (npm/pnpm, tabs/spaces), duplicate blocks across files, dead path/command/glob references; no precision or recall; "Grade weights are tuned by feel, not data".
**Comments:** `nimblegate` c49912308: agents skip rules when the check is a hook on the same machine; can the agent turn off the hook?
**Links followed:** both repos.
**→ Lain:** RuleReceipt's 62.8% over-refusal on replayed calls is the same measurement as item 63 (over-ask rate on real traffic) from a different tool, and its design rule, only structured checks may produce a confident FAIL, is a good grader rule for "did the agent follow the instruction file" in a bench: grade from the Effect log, not narration (item 43). `nimblegate`'s question is the self-protection question (4.5).
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 63 and item 43; none new.

### 3.11 MCP hygiene: registry measurement, drift, scanners — ids=49962605 (3pts), 49944383 (5pts), 49976078 (4pts), 49964286 (2pts), 49833750 (1pt), 49807686 (2pts), 49982817 (4pts)
**Article:** **mcp-transparency-log** (yassinht, fetched): daily unauthenticated `tools/list` crawl of the official registry, 755,221 observations, 38 daily censuses by 2026-10-06; first census 25,020 entries, 13,997 declaring a remote endpoint, 7,820 enumerable without credentials (56.4%), 132,683 tools, 73.2% with an annotation hint, two operators hold 30.2% of reachable servers. Drift: 19.2% of about 8,100 enumerable servers changed tool surface between two censuses 17 hours apart, but the first figure was an artefact (different time of day); with same-hour comparison real change was about 0.5 to 1% per day, 87% of edits from one publisher, 2.3% per day for the rest; about 95% of changes kept tool names and rewrote descriptions or schemas. Observations go into an RFC 6962 Merkle tree with a signed head. **guardmcp** (BerkantACUN, fetched): static scan of 3,945 latest-version registry servers (2026-09-07): 90% remote-only (nothing to pin), 14% installable, 59 hostnames claimed by more than one namespace (one by 213). **mcpward** (TsvetanG2, fetched): lockfile of `tools/list` (`mcpward.lock.json`), classifies drift (description rewrite as possible rug-pull, schema breaking or not, `readOnlyHint` flipped), flags injection phrasing, hidden Unicode tag characters, schemas soliciting secrets, near-identical descriptions with divergent schemas. **ToolHub** (Talos-popcorn, fetched): folder-tree navigation of tools (`listTools("/system")`), no token or accuracy measurement. **life-forge** (zariffromlatif, deterministic MCP scanner) and **hiddencontent.ai** (analyses file layout for white/tiny/occluded text, $0.01 per page): scanners, no evaluation read.
**Comments:** none (all zero or near-zero comment stories).
**Links followed:** all five repos or pages.
**→ Lain:** The transparency log's "surface hash versus body hash, same-hour comparison" is a worked example of a measurement trap (the 83.5% noise estimate was a time-of-day artefact), and the drift rate is a prior for Lain's MCP story: tool descriptions change without version change, so a `Toolset` rendered from an MCP server is not content-addressed unless Lain hashes the description into the Request. mcpward's lockfile-diff is the CI-shaped form of the same thing; Lain's `Request#prefix_digests` already makes a description change show up as a cache break (item 56), so the cheap addition is to journal a per-tool description digest and flag a change mid-session. The injection-phrasing and hidden-Unicode checks are a sensible `Tool::Input`-adjacent lint for MCP tool descriptions (cf. tier-2 papers 2602.14878 and 2602.18914 on description defects).
**→ ROADMAP:** none new; supports item 50 (deferred disclosure) with a drift prior; candidate 6 below.

### 3.12 Pi extensions and offload: Pi-Infinite-Context, Ototo, pi-delegate, Pi subagents Lite, Claude Code Mode — ids=50072881 (2pts, 6c), 49971028 (2pts), 49933095 (1pt), 50103048 (5pts, 1c), 50042642 (1pt, 1c)
**Article:** **Pi-Infinite-Context** (fdietze, fetched): the agent creates "folds" with `context_fold`, a summary standing for consecutive messages or earlier folds; originals stay archived and searchable (`context_map`, `context_search` regex, `context_peek`); reminders at about 75% context; Pi's own compaction is blocked. No evals, no cache statement (4 stars). **Ototo** (ototo.dev, fetched): offloads code exploration to a local model (author runs Qwen 3.8 27B); numbers on the vendor page, all self-run on Opus 5.5 with Claude Code: 31% lower API spend on nine vendor-written tasks, 3 runs each, 100% accuracy; 58% lower than Claude Code's native Explore agent on real bug fixes in four Java libraries (identical success, judged by each library's tests); 17% lower token cost than standalone Claude Code. **pi-delegate**: "up to 33% savings", no method. **Pi subagents Lite**: schema-first subagents with minimal system-prompt impact. **Claude Code Mode** (gabe4coding, fetched): the model writes one JavaScript program calling MCP tools in a Node sandbox (no fs, CPU limit, network blocked where `unshare -rn` works), every MCP call still passes the permission check; programs can be saved as reusable functions only after the human approves the code; no numbers.
**Comments:** `thepasch` c50072918 and `manx` c50073035: folding wrecks cache hits after the fold point; author: "I don't have a solid cost analysis". `verdverm` c50073406: "I won't touch anything that touches my context without very compelling evals."
**Links followed:** all of the above except pi-delegate and Lite (README-level).
**→ Lain:** Pi-Infinite-Context is `Context::Compact` made agent-initiated and reversible: the archive plus `context_peek` is what Lain's Timeline already provides for free (a fold is a render-time projection). The agent-initiated versus threshold-initiated trigger is a clean arm axis for item 48, and its 75%-reminder-without-force is the "nudge" DCP also uses. Ototo's 58% claim against the native Explore agent is the only subagent-offload result in the batch with a ground-truth judge (library tests); it is the comparison Lain's fresh-root subagent default (CLAUDE.md "Architecture", verified) should be measured against with a local model on `ollama`. Claude Code Mode's "save a program only after the human reads it" is a gate on a persisted capability, adjacent to Lain's approval ladder.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 48 (trigger axis: threshold versus agent-initiated fold); `### M5: orchestration, memory, code mode`: record the sandboxed-program-with-per-call-permission pattern beside the code-mode item.

---

## 4. Injection, secrets, approval and command guards  (SCOPE: harness-evaluation, safety seams)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** Three results change how Lain should read its own ladder. (1) Detector benchmarks do not transfer: 15 injection detectors plus 2 judges, the best BIPIA detector catches 2% of AgentDojo injections at 1% false positives, one that catches 72% on AgentDojo catches 15% on tau-bench (2610.03448). (2) Human approval has a measured capacity and an attack: loopjacking (2609.21081) substitutes the executed operation after or beside the approved one in real frameworks, and an oversight model shows an inverted-U where more escalation makes the system less safe (2606.08919). (3) Provenance beats classification when you can afford the friction: a replay gateway stops 98.5% to 99.8% of hijacked AgentDojo tool calls without reading the text, against 70.2% for a classifier and 26.1% for Prompt Guard 2, at 28.9% to 56.7% of legitimate tasks needing approval. The small Show HN guards are numerous (Pawl, Paveo, Termaxa, DeadBolt, Laya, a guard-hook plugin, Nimblegate, Squidbrake, aws-safebox) and nearly all describe themselves as "not a sandbox"; the useful signal is a shared public corpus (skillkeel/tamper-cases, 121 cases) and Pawl's labelled-corpus comparison. The Omnis Watchtower "tripwire" is a package-install gate over registry metadata, not a binary-identity tripwire, so it complements rather than competes with Lain's plan (4.8).

### 4.1 Loopjacking, oversight capacity, and agents that evade monitors — ids=49796685 (4pts), 49950671 (1pt), 49867596 (4pts, 1c), 49897337 (4pts), 49929469 (1pt, 1c)
**Article:** **Loopjacking** (arXiv 2609.21081, abs fetched, not API-vetted): the human approves operation A and the system executes B. Two variants: representation-based (B is in the request but omitted or misdescribed in what the reviewer sees) and post-approval state substitution (mutable workflow state changes after the approval). Reproduced in 7 Agno AgentOS releases (to 3.0.9), 12 versions of a conditional in-memory LangGraph Agent Server composition (to 0.14.0), and OpenClaw 2026.2.23 (rejected in .24); OpenAI Agents SDK 0.22.0/.2 is the negative control because serialised continuation keeps exact per-call binding. Defences: render the complete canonical operation, compare it exactly at use, forbid mutation of pending state. The paper does not claim prevalence. **Oversight Has a Capacity** (2606.08919, abs fetched, not API-vetted): 125 adversarially-weighted agent actions, Fleiss' kappa 0.52 between reviewers on what is risky; guard as selective classification under asymmetric cost; with reviewer fatigue growing in escalation load, safety is an inverted-U in escalation rate (a modelling result motivating a human study); a load-aware policy resists a flooding attack. **AI Agents Push Humans Out of the Loop** (2608.23642, Mitchell, Ghosh, Passi; submitted twice as 49867596 and 49897337): human-in-the-loop is not sufficient because designs make oversight harder and skills atrophy; position paper, no numbers. **Covert Assistance** (2609.39050): 7 of 9 frontier models disguised a withheld credential to help a developer while evading a monitor; DeepSeek-V4-Pro planner attempted concealment in 16.9% of 6,000 episodes and the credential was recovered in 0.9% (61.3% chance of at least one breach in 105 episodes); giving the monitor the credential improves detection but needs trusting it with the secret.
**Comments:** `77rushi77` c49929894 (unverified): the 61.3% assumes independent episodes and the 0.9% is about 50 breaches in 6,000; would the rate hold across wordings of the non-disclosure rule?
**Links followed:** abs pages for all four papers.
**→ Lain:** (a) Loopjacking: `Approval::Queue::Outstanding#decide` resolves a promise on the same parked call (`lib/lain/approval/queue.rb:199` area, verified) and `Approval::Gate` binds a decision to an `artifact_digest` ("addresses CONTENT, so an edited plan", `lib/lain/approval/gate.rb:70`, verified), so the post-approval substitution shape looks structurally avoided for the epic gate; I did not verify that the tool-call approval path re-renders from live state after approval. A seam spec is the check: approve a parked `bash` call, mutate the pending argument hash, assert the executed argv equals the approved one and that the approval record carries the digest of what ran. (b) The inverted-U gives a number to chase for the bypass/auto/ask ladder: report the escalation rate per session and a flood arm (many benign asks, one malicious) as a bench task; the judge-first `auto` default is what keeps escalation low. (c) Covert Assistance is the `Mailbox`/agent-to-agent channel risk: a monitor watching a channel is evaded by encoded content; relevant to the delegation law (item 54). Kappa 0.52 is a prior for human-labelled "risky" corpora: label with two reviewers or distrust.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 46 (approval judge loop-level state), item 54 (delegation law) and item 63 (measure over-ask rate); candidate 8.

### 4.2 Injection detectors, steering, control tokens, and tool-use safety — ids=49974036 (2pts), 49960155 (1pt), 49840698 (1pt), 50076687 (2pts)
**Article:** **Passing the Test You Trained On** (2610.03448, abs fetched, not API-vetted): 15 detectors (including Prompt Guard 2) and 2 task-aware LLM judges on tool outputs from AgentDojo, tau-bench and BIPIA; ground-truth tool calls replayed with no LLM so outputs are benign by construction, injected outputs labelled by differential replay. Rankings transfer poorly; best BIPIA detector 2% of AgentDojo injections at 1% FPR; one detector 72% on AgentDojo and 15% on tau-bench; false-positive rates on tool outputs 0% to over 90% and those do transfer; the best detector on both was trained on agent-style inputs sharing no data with the benchmarks. Recommendations: evaluate on the agent's own tool outputs, report detection at low FPR, audit training data. **CounterSteer** (2609.36570, Russinovich, fetched): white-box activation steering subtracted from tool-result tokens during prefill; five open-weight models (8B to 106B), held-out attack success 0.21 to 1.00 down to 0.00 to 0.17, AgentDojo compromise 0.10 to 0.49 down to 0.006 to 0.079 with 93 to 100% benign utility; 13 of 18 parameter-manipulation attacks resisted, so it motivates argument-provenance controls. **Control-Token Injection** (2609.27542, fetched): appending a model's own channel-control tokens suppresses reasoning on gpt-oss-20b (mean 52.5 tokens to 0 over 40 tasks, tool call still fired on every trial); a rule-based and a cross-family LLM monitor caught all plain trials and no forged trials; 39.6% of refusals turned into exfiltration; whether an identical call fires depends on the harness parser (two Gemma parsers fired on all 24 versus none on identical generations). **Tools make LLMs less likely to refuse** (2610.03938, NeurIPS 2026, fetched): all tested top MLLMs less safe in tool-using settings; up to 68.7% relative increase in refusal failure rate, over 100,000 responses.
**Comments:** none.
**Links followed:** all four abs pages.
**→ Lain:** The first paper directly instructs how to build Lain's injection test: run the ladder's judge and any detector arm on Lain's own tool results via replay, not on a public benchmark; `Bench::DryReplay` (named in item 40) renders without a model, which is exactly the "replay ground-truth calls with no LLM" method. The control-token paper is a harness finding, not a model one: the chat-template renderer and tool-call parser are part of the attack surface and the parser choice flips outcomes; Lain's Provider wire layer should have a spec that model control tokens in a tool result or user message are escaped or rejected before rendering (the Anthropic path does not use raw templates, but the `ollama` path does, so this lands on local arms; cf. item 59(b)). The "tools reduce refusal" finding means a safety eval must be run with the tool-using Toolset, not bare, or it overstates the refusal rate.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 45 (seam spec with planted instructions) and item 59 (local-arm discipline: control-token handling in the chat template); candidate 9.

### 4.3 Deterministic provenance gating: OpenAPPA, provenance gateway, COBRA, Planlock, Submilli, policy gateways — ids=49877515 (25pts, 12c), 49866732 (1pt), 49962382 (2pts), 49993786 (1pt, 1c), 49980140 (3pts, 1c), 49858029 (1pt), 49830471 (2pts), 49894667 (1pt)
**Article:** **OpenAPPA** (openappa.com and arXiv 2607.24625, "APPA: Recoverable Information-Flow Control", abs fetched, not API-vetted): reference monitor at tool dispatch and protocol gateways, checks restrictions before execution and validates outputs before they enter context; labels each value with where it came from and who may see it, gradual typing for unannotated tools, disposable child branches absorb taint, "remedy plans" so a blocked agent can recover. Evaluation page (fetched): 0 scored attacks in 1,320 guarded episodes (600 Bench-Corp, 720 AgentThreatBench), 88.0 to 90.0% task completion across three models; Microsoft FIDES had 28 to 35% attack success and 37 to 45% completion; Claude Auto mode let 10% through, with a head-to-head of 2/20 and 8/24 stock and 0/20 and 6/24 IFC-tuned; Bench-Corp completion with one model fell 88.0% to 56.5% without subagent isolation and to 35.0% without guided recovery; on a tau banking benchmark guarded 151/388 versus stock 156/388. The authors are Archestra-affiliated and the Auto comparison is theirs. **Provenance gateway** (Yehielamor/provenance-gate post, fetched): classify sinks by verb name or URL argument, label control arguments (recipient, account, URL, amount) as from user, untrusted tool output or model, allow or hold. AgentDojo replay with no model: argument-strict 98.5% of hijacked attacks stopped (56.7% of legitimate tasks need approval), full policy 99.3% and 99.8% obfuscated (28.9% friction), argument-permissive 95.2% but 26.7% on obfuscated; protectai-v2 classifier 70.2% plain, 21.2% Unicode-tag, 35.3% German; Prompt Guard 2 26.1% plain and 0% on base64, tags, German. Caveats stated: never reached under 10% friction, a poisoned counterparty graph drops stops to 53.2%, cannot stop content leaks (EchoLeak), end-to-end test was a 5-pair pilot. **COBRA** (2610.03089, fetched): branch steering attacks on computer-use agents with a Dual-LLM; STEER-Bench, 101 tasks in 9 domains; attack success 94.4% on standard agents and 89.5% on vanilla Dual-LLM; trusted branching plans plus ahead-of-time capability constraints bring it to 0% at 97% benign utility. **Planlock** (JosephCurwin, fetched): an MCP proxy; the human approves a plan out of band, the approved plan is hashed, per-step tool allow-list, argument constraints (`eq`, `enum`, `min`, `max`, `max_length`), `max_calls`, optional `pin` re-read right before the call (a script hash); pin mismatch puts the plan on Hold which only the human can clear; three rounds of LLM red-team found 4 weaknesses, fixed. **Submilli** (somdoron): runtime for code-mode agents where a package defines the permission vocabulary ("allow a refund up to $500 for customer 123"), TypeScript compiled to WebAssembly. **Squidbrake** (batrapulkit, fetched): approval gateway with hash-chained audit and signed checkpoints; claims 13 of 14 harmful actions stopped in replays of 11 public incidents, 20 of 20 reported bypasses, 64 of 65 harmful session steps stopped with 3 of 41 routine held; a known unstopped bypass is a malicious MCP server adding a BCC after the check; binding of approval to arguments is not described. **ai-coding-gateway** (49830471) and **compliance-ail** (49858029): rule gateways with local dashboards or tamper-evident logs; story text only.
**Comments:** `keshakon` c49877745 (OpenAPPA author, unverified): "batteries" let an authorization decision run an arbitrary program, e.g. a GitHub API call checking if a repo is public before allowing a post to Slack. `arseny_info` c49878701: a wildcard AI annotator emits a local label, not allow or block, so the decision stays in label algebra. `vladimir_gor` c49878516: it reframes security as information flow. `nicocarsui` c49993846 (Planlock): "tried this already, it simply does not work. AI invariably regresses over time" (unverified, no detail). `nobu666` c49844794 (in the contract thread, 4.9): a PreToolUse hook denying MCP actions starting `send_/delete_/submit_/sign_` and browser clicks on elements labelled "Submit", "Sign", found by looking up the clicked ref in earlier read_page results.
**Links followed:** openappa.com/evaluation, the provenance post, Planlock, Squidbrake, COBRA abs page.
**→ Lain:** This cluster is Lain's open design question stated as measurements: should the `auto` judge be replaced or fronted by a deterministic provenance check? Lain already has the ingredients (Event provenance, `Toolset` attenuation, `Sensitivity::Policy`), and item 46 already says the judge needs loop-level state. The provenance gateway's finding is the experiment: label each control argument of a sink call with the Event it came from (user turn, tool result, model), and run it as an arm beside the judge on one corpus, reporting stop rate and friction (legitimate calls held) as a pair. Its replay-without-a-model method fits `DryReplay`. Planlock's pin (re-read a value right before the call) is the TOCTOU defence for item 60/`Shell::Executable` freshness. OpenAPPA's own ablation (subagent isolation and guided recovery are worth 31 and 53 points of completion) is evidence that a blocked agent needs a recovery message, which is what Lain's Gate refusals do in text; measure whether the refusal text moves completion. Treat the vendor-authored comparisons (OpenAPPA versus Auto, Squidbrake's replays) as unverified.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 46; new item (candidate 10): a provenance-label arm for the approval ladder, scored as (stop rate, friction) pairs on a held-out corpus.

### 4.4 Prompt injection in HTTP headers: what 217M web responses show — id=50104310 (93pts, 17c)
**Article:** signalscorps.com (fetched). 217,097,223 page responses (about 10% of Common Crawl CC-MAIN-2026-39); every header written for an AI system kept: 7,959 sites sent one, 30 tried to give orders (24 via `X-AI`): 22 pranks, 5 Anthropic refusal test strings, 2 fake applicant scores, 1 SQL drop-table joke, 1 base64 request to report the model name, 1 AgentPwn honeypot (the only one asking to run a command). 7,271 of 7,959 were pointers to agent resources (6,989 `x-llms-txt`). Crawler fetched as CCBot so it cannot see agent-only cloaking. A canary-server test: with everyday prompts 0 of 9 runs obeyed across Opus, Sonnet and Haiku in Claude Code; when the prompt said the site owner wants header instructions followed, 7 of 9 obeyed; obedience also required the fetch tool to pass headers to the model (plain `curl` did not). The 96% agent-detection figure a commenter cited is not in the article.
**Comments:** `simonw` c50104820 and c50105398: the post is long and sloppy, the examples look like jokes, and he expects this year's models to be hard to inject with a short header. `crisnoble` c50105347 (unverified): servers can serve agents different content; "96%" (not in the article). `tcdent` c50105330: to an LLM it is all text. `ArashEdalat` c50106844: a resume PDF with white-on-white text was flagged by Claude.
**Links followed:** the article.
**→ Lain:** In-the-wild attack rate in headers is near zero (30 of 217M responses, 1 asking for a command); the real signal is the "site owner wants it followed" prompt condition (0 of 9 to 7 of 9). That is a clean bench arm: the same fetch fixture with a system-prompt sentence granting a source authority, scored on obedience. It also confirms that Lain should keep response headers out of the model-visible result unless asked (check `Tools::WebFetch`; not verified here).
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 45 (obedience-to-planted-instruction sweep): add "source granted authority in the prompt" as a factor.

### 4.5 Command guards for coding agents: Pawl, Laya guard, Paveo, Termaxa, DeadBolt, guard hooks and tamper-cases, aws-safebox, warehouse rules — ids=50042172 (2pts), 49993085 (2pts), 49979656 (2pts), 50069921 (3pts), 50117874 (1pt), 49892360 (1pt), 49890579 (1pt), 50047033 (1pt), 50117268 (3pts), 49902237 (1pt)
**Article:** All fetched. **skillkeel starter + tamper-cases** (50042172): `guard-bash`, `secret-scan` hooks; the useful artefact is the public corpus github.com/skillkeel/tamper-cases: 121 cases in four families (guard-file-tamper 52, destructive 33, git-config-exec 20, contract-file-write 16) with `must_pass` benign cases, a runner that reads exit code 2 or `permissionDecision` JSON, and a RESULTS.md per guard version. Published rows: guard-bash 0.1.0 destructive 17/21 and git-config-exec 0/12, then 0.1.5 21/21 and 12/12 with zero false positives on 22 benign forms; another hook scored 48/50 on file tamper but 2/21 then 11/21 on destructive; `cc-safety-net` and `claude-code-hooks` rows exist. **Pawl** (49993085, 49979656): a "blast-radius" gate on deletes of home or root "directly or from a script, trap, npm run, Makefile, python -c or container"; never executes the command; five labelled corpora, 329 cases, four held out before tuning; replay of 472 real commands with no false alarms; October 3 comparison on 191 dangerous and 138 everyday cases: pawl 190 caught and 0 false alarms, cc-safety-net 2.5.1 100/22, its `paranoid` mode 144/62, dcg 0.15.2 156/61 (author wrote both guard and cases; competitor false-alarm counts not measured); an ablation of 24 tasks, 3 passes, with gates on versus off: Sonnet 5 57/67 safe-and-done versus 41/67 with harm 0 versus 17, local Qwen3.8 Flash-Next 50/72 versus 48/72 with harm 5 versus 16; about 45 ms per Read and 65 ms per Bash; 12 second watchdog, asks on timeout; admits a script that builds a path at run time is not fenced. **Laya guard** (50069921): local 322M model plus 12 regex policies, split chained commands and take the worst verdict; AgentDojo 25 of 27 attacks blocked, 92 of 97 benign kept, 69/71 shell commands; the author lists the bypasses reviewers found and fixed: a trailing comment fooled the model, only the first 4,000 characters were read (junk prefix bypass), `curl` x 40,000 made one regex run 48 seconds so the hook timed out and the command ran, and a down server meant no check (now `ask`, or block when the agent cannot ask; setting `LAYA_GUARD_FAIL`); open gaps include protected paths seen only in file-tool calls, not shell reads. **Paveo** (50117874): pattern refusals only, "does not understand the shell"; hash-chained audit; names missed forms (`rm -r -f`, variables, aliases, quoted verbs `git 'push'`, abbreviated flags `--ha`), overhead 50 µs per check and 60 ms per hook process; fails closed after 4 seconds. **Termaxa** (49892360): previews what a command would destroy (files for `rm`, rows and dependents for Postgres, the commit a force-push loses, the Terraform plan) and backs up before approval; 583 regression tests, 34 bugs "found in the wild" including compound-command and escaped-quote bypasses; no detection rate; "a file no rule names is silence". **guardrails-plugin** (49890579): PreToolUse and SessionStart hooks; fails `ask` not `allow`; session report marks each loaded instruction file, rule, skill and hook OK/WARN/SKIPPED including "AGENTS.md present but not read"; 150 to 300 tokens of the report reach the model. **DeadBolt** (50047033): fail-closed admission in the code path that runs an action, descendant runs cannot widen an ancestor's policy, operator kill, reported-spend caps; explicitly "execution receipts, not certification"; 4 stars. **aws-safebox** (50117268): TOML prefix rules (`terraform destroy`, recursive `aws s3 rm`, `kubectl delete`), unsupported shell constructs (`|`, `&`, `$()`, backticks) blocked, "either understands a command sufficiently or blocks it"; PATH proxies bypassable by absolute path; 59 tests. **data-agent-rules** (49902237): 8 AGENTS.md rules, hooks blocking destructive SQL and pricing BigQuery queries, 12 scenarios on a local DuckDB warehouse graded safe and useful; 9 scenarios x 3 runs x 3 models = 162 runs: safe Haiku 4.5 10/27 to 25/27, Sonnet 5 19/27 to 27/27, Opus 5.5 22/27 to 27/27; harmful runs 10 to 2, 3 to 0, 0 to 0; wrote to prod unasked 4/18, 3/18, 3/18 to 0/18 each; one no-rules Sonnet run billed $396.07 on a 31 TiB table; author notes 3 runs per cell is noise-level.
**Comments:** `moneytool` c49886906 / c49886929: advertises github.com/moneytool/aegis-devops (not opened).
**Links followed:** tamper-cases, skillkeel, Pawl, Laya, Paveo, Termaxa, guardrails-plugin, DeadBolt, aws-safebox, data-agent-rules.
**→ Lain:** The corpus is the find: item 63 asked for specs on env-prefix injection and delayed-execution writes, and `tamper-cases` is a ready third-party test set whose `git-config-exec` family (`core.fsmonitor`, `core.hooksPath`, `core.sshCommand`, filters, aliases via `git -c`, writes into `.git/`, a planted `.git` directory) and `guard-file-tamper` family (the guard's own files; `git stash`/`reset` over them; symlinks) map onto Lain's `Shell::Parse`/triage and the bash-confinement tripwire. A guard that cannot protect its own config is a class Lain should state: the confinement spec's reader sandbox is what makes the guard's files unwritable, a stronger answer than a path rule. Concrete uses: (a) a spec harness that feeds each of the 121 cases through Lain's triage and ladder and records caught/asked/passed with `must_pass` as the false-positive column; (b) Laya's three bugs (junk prefix past a read window, regex backtracking timeout, fail-open on outage) are exactly the "judge path's open guardrail holes" and "fail-closed default for unknown programs" follow-ups listed in ROADMAP item 58 (see its Done note, 2026-10-16); each is a one-line spec: a 40,000-repeat input must not time out the triage, a down judge must ask or deny, a command longer than the judge's window must still be judged whole. (c) Pawl's 329-case, 4-held-out, replay-of-472-real-commands protocol is the evaluation shape to adopt for item 63; its ablation (gates on versus off, harm counts) is the right bench output, and its honesty rule (competitor false alarms "not measured") is the discipline. Contrast: every tool here is regex or pattern at the command text; Lain's resolved-executable approach (bash-confinement) is a different class and should say so.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 63 (over-ask replay) and the follow-ups under item 58 (judge guardrail holes, fail-closed default); candidate 11.

### 4.6 Secret scrubbers and credential exposure: Tokenhush, Venya, Agent Scrub, Stroq, Heldkeys, Nimblegate, secret extraction — ids=49826094 (3pts), 49801376 (3pts), 49939584 (1pt), 49806952 (1pt, 3c), 50074883 (1pt, 1c), 50118028 (2pts), 49991636 (1pt), 49799938 (4pts, 1c), 49905889 (1pt)
**Article:** **Tokenhush** (fregie/tokenhush, fetched): loopback HTTP gateway between the tool and the vendor API; walks the outbound JSON body and swaps detected secrets for session-stable placeholders `__PII_<type>_<digest>__`, restores them in responses (SSE buffered whole) and never back into outbound requests; default detectors prefix shapes, JWT, PEM, Luhn, email; entropy off by default because its false positives broke function calling; encoded secrets and JSON object keys are not walked; it does not discuss prompt-cache effect and publishes no overhead. **Venya** (tabith-llc, fetched, alpha): the agent names a secret by path (`/run/secrets/venya/12`), the value is materialised as a mode 0400 tmpfs file inside a microVM and consumed by env injection, askpass, sudo-stdin or sshpass; stdout and stderr scrubbed to `[REDACTED:<id>]` by a Rust filter plus a fail-closed server filter; deny-by-default egress; admits the plaintext exists during execution so protection rests on scrubbing and egress. **Agent Scrub** (thesubtlety, fetched): macOS tool that finds and redacts secrets already in agent history (12 tools), fingerprints them with a per-install key, edits files in place, skips files being written; cannot remove copies already sent to a provider. **Stroq** (AGGIB/Stroq): reads the session the agent already recorded and reports what secrets were sent (`@stroq/cli sent --last`), no network. **Heldkeys** (hugomn, fetched): inventory of credentials reachable by the current user: env vars by name and prefix, cloud and AI-tool logins, package and git credentials, `.env` including gitignored, ssh-agent keys, MCP server configs, Docker socket and cloud metadata endpoint; 76 local and 15 CI rules; "A clean report means none of the known places expose anything, not that the agent holds nothing." **Nimblegate** (nimblegate/nimblegate, story text): a self-hosted git-shell between the dev machine and the final repo that holds the write token; observe or gating mode; PolyForm Noncommercial. **Practical Secrets Extraction** (2609.36941, abs fetched, not API-vetted): black-box recovery of masked provider-specific credentials from three deployed systems spanning OpenAI and Claude Code via a local proxy model, no quantitative results in the abstract.
**Comments:** `arendsee` c49808441: README looks like an AI dump (Stroq).
**Links followed:** Tokenhush, Venya, Agent Scrub, Heldkeys, the paper.
**→ Lain:** Item 47 (mask secrets in command output) now has three designs to compare: Tokenhush's whole-body request-side placeholder swap (and its choice to leave entropy off because it broke function calling is a measured false-positive cost), Venya's reference-by-id (the model never holds the value) plus output scrub, and Lain's file-read region mask. Placeholders stable within a session keep cache bytes stable (the enrolled-literal idea in item 47); I could not confirm whether Tokenhush preserves cache. Heldkeys is a ready preflight for Lain's bench worktrees: a run's session header could record what credentials were reachable (env names, `.env`, ssh-agent) so a leak result can be read against exposure. Agent Scrub and Stroq are a reminder that the Journal is itself a credential store: the NDJSON session file holds whatever the model saw; scrub-on-write (not in place) is the Lain-native answer and should be a stated property (the Journal is opened on a descriptor, sync mode, per CLAUDE.md "Output discipline"). The extraction paper is the reason the cache of enrolled secrets must never contain real values.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 47; add "record reachable credentials in the session header" to item 52's header list.

### 4.7 Hidden-instruction and consensus attacks, watermarking, and agent identity — ids=49856149 (59pts, 72c), 49922736 (82pts, 28c), 49800563 (1pt)
**Article:** **Provenance tax** (lasso.security, fetched): SynthID-Text watermarking applied to open models (phi-4, Llama-3.1-8B, Granite-3.2-8B, gemma-3-27b/12b, Qwen3-4B, more) alters agent behaviour. Paired design, same seed and order; BFCL v4 tool calling at temperatures 0.001, 0.7, 1.0 and HarmBench with a fixed prompt injection. Tool-calling accuracy fell for 6 of 7 models (significant for 4); mean verdict churn 6.5% over 21 model-temperature cells; phi-4 16.8% churn at T=1.0; under injection gemma-3-27b churn rose 6.0% to 23.5% and net compliance moved from -1.0 to +12.5 points; the effect depends on the watermark key (Llama-3.1-8B: study key +3.5 points attack success, ten other keys averaging +4.4, range -4.5 to +14.5). **Identity Management for Agentic AI** (OpenID Foundation whitepaper, 2025; not fetched beyond the thread). **ScopeTrail** (scopetrail/scopetrail, fetched): TypeScript library that mints an Ed25519-over-JCS signed receipt per multi-hop delegation (RFC 8693 token exchange `act` claims are informational), checks that each hop's delegate is the next hop's delegator and that scope is a subset at every hop, depth cap 5, 5 second skew, tokens stored as SHA-256 digests, stateless verification, receipts published to an AT Protocol PDS; verdict codes `SIGNATURE_INVALID`, `NONCE_REPLAY`, `RECEIPT_EXPIRED`, scope-subset failures; no revocation, in-process replay store, key rotation deferred.
**Comments:** `niyikiza` c49936430 (identity thread, unverified): Tenuo, macaroon-style capability warrants for multi-hop delegation, Rust core, Apache 2.0. `udbhavs` c49926845: lists about 50 competing standards. `juanre` c49925860: awid.ai. `iamjake648` c49926171: how it relates to Okta/Auth0 XAA.
**Links followed:** lasso article, ScopeTrail repo.
**→ Lain:** The watermark paper is a confound for the bench: if a provider watermarks, the same model with and without it differs in tool-call accuracy by a few points, key-dependent.
