## 9A. Model launches: Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Haiku 5.5  (SCOPE: harness-evaluation, optimization, context)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** The 2,200 comments are mostly taste (Opus 5 prose, "pacing the frontier", cyber blocks), but the three launch posts and the platform docs behind them are the densest harness material of the window, because every launch changed the wire contract Lain's Anthropic provider speaks. (1) Effort is now the only thinking control: Opus 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 default to `medium`, thinking cannot be disabled on Opus 5.5, `thinking: disabled` 400s at xhigh/max on Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 adds `thinking: {type: "between_tools"}`, and "max" can spend the whole 128k output cap on thinking and return nothing (simonw, Sonnet 5.5, 128,000 thinking tokens, 15m40s, no SVG). (2) Changing top-level `effort` between requests restarts the prompt cache; a per-message `role: system` effort message (beta `mid-conversation-output-config-2026-07-01`) keeps it. (3) Preserved thinking makes a conversation append-only: a change to `system`, `tools` or any earlier message makes the next request 400 for accounts created on or after 2026-08-31, and Opus 5.5 rejects forced `tool_choice`. (4) Classifier refusals arrive as HTTP 200 with `stop_reason: "refusal"` plus `stop_details.category`; Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 can fall back server-side (the docs now answer the question Lain's `StreamAssembler` comment left open), Haiku 5.5 cannot. (5) Prices moved under Lain's `PriceBook` again: Opus 5.5 is $4/$20 with $0.20 cache reads (5% of input), Sonnet 5.5 is $2/$10 with $0.10 cache reads after a post-launch cut, Haiku 5.5 is $0.10/$0.50 up to 100k prompt tokens and 5x above, on a tokenizer that counts about 30% more tokens than Haiku 4.5. (6) Benchmark-methodology material is thin but sharp: scores are published at the best effort per model (Opus at xhigh, Sonnet at xhigh because "max" scored lower), safeguard fallbacks are counted as failures in some suites and reported through in others, and token use per task, not price per token, is what the third-party charts disagree about.

### 9.1 Claude Opus 5.5 — id=49803892 (1806pts, 1119c)
**Article:** (WebFetch of anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5, plus the platform docs it links.) Model id `claude-opus-5-5`. Price per MTok: input $4, output $20, cache read $0.20, cache write $5 (Opus 5: $5/$25/$0.50/$6.25); cache hits and refreshes are 0.05x base input. "About 40% less" than Opus 5 at default settings; output more than 30% faster; fast mode up to 2.5x speed at $8/$40. Default effort is `medium` (Opus 5 defaulted to `high`), five levels low/medium/high/xhigh/max. Headline benchmarks use adaptive thinking at max effort "unless noted"; Terminal-Bench 4.0 is Opus at xhigh, standard error ±2.6 points, public leaderboard 5 trials per task through the Claude Code harness; Terminal-Bench-Science standard error ±3.5 to 5 points, 3 trials. AutomationBench was run "without fallback models, so safeguard interventions counted as failures"; production safeguards were on elsewhere, so cyber tasks went to Opus 4.8 and bio/frontier-LLM tasks to Opus 5, "which likely lowers Opus 5.5's scores". WANDR ran with offline web tools, programmatic tool calling, code execution and a 980k-token task budget. Thinking cannot be turned off; preserved-thinking guard against distillation applies to API accounts created on or after 2026-08-31. The system card is a 10MB+ PDF that WebFetch refused (not read). From the platform docs (all article-sourced numbers, not comments): `thinking: disabled`, `budget_tokens`, forced `tool_choice` (`any`/named tool), non-default `temperature`/`top_p`/`top_k` and assistant prefill all 400 on `claude-opus-5-5`; text between tool calls now arrives as empty "progress-update" `thinking` blocks unless `thinking.display` is `"updates"` (beta `thinking-display-updates-2026-08-18`) or `"summarized"`; the 1M window needs no beta header; at `xhigh`/`max` the docs recommend `max_tokens` 64k to 128k because thinking counts against it. The prompting page adds three harness patterns: a text-only `end_turn` mid-task is a report, not completion (re-prompt naming open checklist items, stop after 2 or 3 continuations); elapsed-time lines such as `elapsed 340s / 1200s` make multi-agent teams finish sooner at comparable quality; a turn-scoped system message (`clear_at: "next_user_message"`) reminding the model to speak after 5 silent tool steps "roughly halved" long silent stretches with no cost change (Anthropic's own testing).
**Comments:** all unverified.
- `GodelNumbering` c49804160, `alvis` c49804051, `benjiro29` c49804440: the price table; the cache-read cut is the biggest lever. `bleonard` c49805358 reads it as pushing longer agent threads (cache read 5% vs 10%). `Espressosaurus` c49804490 (95 to 98% of tokens are cache reads in interactive sessions) and `hedgehog` c49805086 (98 to 99% on experiment loops, so Fable 5.1 cost "about the same as Opus 5" once cache reads dominate) give the measured share. `weiran` c49804411 / `alvis` c49804230 note subscription limits rose only about 25%.
- `drbscl` c49804768/c49804733: Artificial Analysis shows Opus 5.5 "even more verbose" at max, similar cost per task, so the 40% claim is "exaggerated". `jsnell` c49804888 and `persedes` c49818118 answer with AA's per-effort cost table: Opus 5 mean $4.135 / median $4.245 vs 5.5 mean $3.150 / median $2.640 across medium to max, so about a 38% median saving; `nl` c49809918: 5.5 uses 38% more tokens at max, 21% fewer at high, lower at medium/low. `qlte` c49805459 quotes AA cost per task: Opus 5.5 medium $1.34, high $1.82; GPT-6 Astra high $1.76. The lesson is the claim moves with the effort at which it is measured.
- `Sonnet thread, oh_no` c49882650 (AA max-effort output tokens per task: Astra 27k, Fable 5.1 78k, Sonnet 5 118k, Opus 5.5 119k, Sonnet 5.5 193k) and `usef-` c49884349 (table: Opus medium 51 score / $1.34, high 54 / $1.82, max 58 / $5.98; Sonnet medium 41 / $0.59, high 47 / $1.08, max 56 / $7.60).
- `gwd` c49808623: his own mailing-list patch review harness, 12 patches with 14 planted issues: Opus 5.5 found 8/14 for $15.40, Fable 5.1 7/14 for $66.34, Opus 5 6/14 (corrected to $58.19), Sonnet 5 2/14 for $19.15; costs are "equivalent API" from the Claude CLI on a subscription. Same harness held fixed across four models, n=1 run. `roflc0ptic` c49810352: back-testing against PR comments, Opus 5.5 matched Fable 5.1, about $24 for 10 PRs. `gwd` c49812969: prompts were iterated by Fable/Opus 5.5 against failing runs; the "single prompt" variant produced these numbers.
- `rmunn` c49810898: Claude Code had silently reverted to "Effort: medium" mid-session; the review hallucinated line numbers (line 848 in a 160-line file). Effort drift inside the harness is a confound.
- `xgb84j` c49883791 (Sonnet thread): in Claude Code "sub agents inherit the thinking level", so a cheaper subagent needs a different model; `mnicky` c49884808 says custom agents can pin an effort. `walthamstow` c50004154 (Haiku thread): subagents inherit the parent model unless told otherwise.
- `solsson` c49815025 + gist c49818208: Opus 5.5, auto mode, effort high, ran `pkill -f "cat" -U $(id -u) -n` to stop a hung `cat`; on macOS option parsing stops at the first operand so `-U`/`-n` became patterns and about 144 processes matched. The postmortem was written by a Sonnet reading the transcript. `data-ottawa` c49815083: the model edited production data models without the usual confirm.
- `Retr0id` c49805157 / c49810011: the fallback banner ("Opus 4.8 is answering instead, or you can edit and retry"), and innocuous bash commands flagged `[Third-Party Attack]`, apparently tied to command auto-approval; manual approval avoided it. `to11mtm` c49901375 (Sonnet thread): the fallback to 4.8 mid-session "murdered my usage quota" and gave no warning. `mintik` c49808591, `johnmlussier` c49881989: the `[cyber]` classifier blocks embedded/driver work even for CVP members; `solenoid0937` c49882294 quotes the CVP page: it did not yet cover Opus 5.5.
- `toephu2` c49806555: compaction fires often at xhigh even on the 1M window; `mnicky` c49807749: since Opus 5 medium/high is enough. `knapcio` c49813143: his own 10-task benchmark varied by up to 50% in completion time between runs of the same model, so "you'd need hundreds of tasks"; this is the variance point in anecdotal form.
- `jwpapi`-style taste complaints (Opus 5 verbosity, "claudisms") are dropped; `akhilome` c49804401 reports a per-turn `UserPromptSubmit` hook reminder tamed it, which is the reminder-cadence axis already in item 51.
**Links followed:** anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5 (read). platform.claude.com docs: effort, preserved-thinking, refusals-and-fallback, mid-conversation-system-messages, task-budgets, compaction-threshold, prompting-claude-opus-5-5, opus-5-5/migration-guide (all read; they are the real payload). arcprize.org/results/anthropic-claude-opus-5-5: ARC-AGI-2 semi-private by effort: low 70.1%, medium 87.5%, high 93.3%, xhigh 92.5%, max 91.7%; ARC-AGI-1: 88.5 / 97.5 / 98.5 / 97.5 / 97.5, so the score peaks at high and falls at xhigh/max. partforge.ai/blog/2026-09-23-new-model-day: 26 CAD tasks x 3 attempts per model, two reviewer models plus manual ranking; Opus 5.5 about 40% cheaper per task than Opus 5, 1.5 vs 4.5 min model time, $0.72 per solved task vs Gemini Flash 3.8 at $0.34; the author says the harness changed between runs so before/after is "a general trend" only. gist solsson (read). github.com/manuelschipper/nah (read): a Rust PreToolUse hook that parses each call into typed effects against 48 guards (30 default), verdicts only "block" or "delegate", never approves, no LLM; it cites Anthropic's own figure that the auto-mode classifier "misses 17% of real overeager actions" (unverified). github.com/deepseek-ai/deepseek-harness (read): MIT, "everything-is-a-plugin" on the Cordis framework, developer preview; `docs/architecture.md` is 404 so nothing on loop or compaction. bench.killswitch-lang.org (JS page, empty to the fetcher). opusfived.dev (a joke page, no data). xen-review-prompts (README did not render). The Opus 5.5 system card PDF exceeded the fetch size cap.
**→ Lain:** Three seams, all verified in WT. (a) `lib/lain/price_book.rb` `DEFAULTS` (lines 42 to 44) hold opus 5/25/6.25/0.5, sonnet 3/15/3.75/0.3, haiku 1/5/1.25/0.1 by family token, so `claude-opus-5-5` prices 25% high on input/output and 150% high on cache reads, and `claude-sonnet-5-5` prices 50% high on input; the file's own header says "Reviewed 2026-08-18" and names single-rate-per-model as a known gap. Cache reads are 95 to 99% of tokens in long sessions (`Espressosaurus`, `hedgehog`), so the cache-read row, not input/output, drives the bench's headline cost. (b) `lib/lain/provider/anthropic_encoding.rb` lines 36 to 50 turn `structured_output` into a forced `tool_choice` at a named tool; `claude-opus-5-5` 400s on that (migration guide, article-sourced), so the structured-output path needs a model-capability gate, not just the thinking one (`thinking_payload.rb` lines 33 to 54 gate on `model.reasoning_option("effort")` but never emit `display`, and `adaptive_thinking_payload` always sends `type: "adaptive"`, which is right for 5.5). (c) `lib/lain/provider/anthropic/stream_assembler.rb` lines 86 to 98 say a mid-stream model switch "is not something to assume in either direction" and that Lain "cannot reach it today". The refusals-and-fallback doc now settles it: pre-output, `message_start` names the fallback model and a `fallback` block is the first content block; mid-output, `message_start` already named the requested model, the open block closes, a `{"type":"fallback","from":{model},"to":{model}}` block marks the boundary, and the per-attempt record lives in the final `message_delta`'s `usage.iterations` (a `fallback_message` entry); sticky routing (about 1 hour, per organization) can start a later turn on the fallback with no `fallback` block at all and `message_start.model` already the fallback. When the day comes, `StreamAssembler`'s reset must key off that `fallback` block, and `thinking`/client `tool_use` blocks before the last `fallback` block must be dropped on replay, `fallback` kept in place. Separately, `lib/lain/stop_reason.rb` already knows `:refusal` (line 50) but `Response` drops `stop_details.category` (grep for `stop_details` in `lib/` finds nothing); the category matters because `bio`, `frontier_llm` and `reasoning_extraction` refusals are billed even with no output while `cyber`/`general_harms` are not. Experiments: (1) effort-sweep arm: ARC's table (peak at high) and the AA tables (max costs 3x to 4x high for +4 score points) say effort belongs on the cost-at-matched-success frontier, with the default explicit per arm because Opus 5.5 moved the default from `high` to `medium`; the confound `rmunn` hit (the harness silently resetting effort) means the Journal should record the effort actually sent. (2) cache-keep effort changes: a `Workspace` or `Context` arm that varies effort mid-run must use the per-message system form or it restarts the cache and the cost comparison is contaminated; this extends item 56. (3) the `solsson` incident is a ready scenario for the approval ladder: a `pkill -f` with a short common pattern and a per-OS option-order trap; shell triage should treat `pkill`/`killall` with `-f` as needing a `pgrep` dry-run count, which is the postmortem's recommendations 2 and 3.
**→ ROADMAP:** `### M3c: the bench: algebra, seams, graders` (and the 3x-too-high Opus cost note near line 1499 under `## Near-term sequence`): extends it; the PriceBook is stale again and needs per-model rows plus a long-context tier. Provider wire items are "new item" (candidates 1, 2, 3 below). Extends item 56 (cache meter vs bill) and item 58 (provider drift vs harness drift: record the served `model` and the `usage.iterations` hops).

### 9.2 Sonnet 5.5 — id=49881850 (884pts, 612c)
**Article:** (WebFetch of anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5.) Model id `claude-sonnet-5-5`. Price per MTok: input $2, output $10, cache read $0.10 (a 50% cut from Sonnet 5's $0.20), cache write $2.50; "up to 30% less per task than Sonnet 5". The Artificial Analysis page fetched later still prints cache read $0.2, so the cut landed after launch; the Haiku thread has `sfkgtbor` c49996490 "Happy about the Sonnet cache read price cut" and `minimaxir` c49996596 "Sonnet 5.5 made zero sense to use over Opus 5.5 under the old cache prices" (unverified). Defaults: `medium` in Claude Code and the apps, `high` on the Platform. Terminal-Bench 4.0 70.6% (Opus 5.5 66.4% at xhigh), FrontierCode v1.1 52.1% at xhigh (Opus 54.4%), CursorBench 55.5% (Opus 57.8%). Methodology notes the page itself gives: Sonnet at max scored lower than at xhigh on FrontierCode because "at Max, Sonnet 5.5 ran Claude Code's code-review skill more often, causing timeouts or extra edits in two cases" (a harness skill, not the model, moved the score); GDPval-AA/AA-Briefcase were run by Artificial Analysis on a pre-release build with a structured-outputs bug; "trials, harness, standard errors: not stated". Lovable reports a third fewer tool calls and about half the shell runs; Sonnet 5.5 batches tool calls more than Sonnet 5. Safeguards: first Sonnet with cyber fallback, higher-risk requests "visibly fall back to Sonnet 5". Preserved thinking is bound to the account that created it, which the page says affects switching accounts mid-session in Claude Code. Artificial Analysis's own write-up (WebFetch): Sonnet 5.5 fell back in about 0.1% of Intelligence Index tasks, "mostly Terminal-Bench 4.0, always to Sonnet 5", with Anthropic's default fallback enabled; max effort uses about 193k output tokens per task (about 60% above Opus 5.5 max) at $7.60 per task; Terminal-Bench 4.0 64% there vs the vendor's 70.6%, so the same model scores 6.6 points apart on different harnesses and settings. The system card PDF exceeded the fetch cap.
**Comments:** all unverified.
- `simonw` c49882573: five efforts on one SVG prompt: low 1,623 output / 0 thinking / 10s; medium 1,796 / 0 / 11s; high 2,334 / 745 / 17s; xhigh 5,730 / 2,535 / 42s; max hit the 128,000 output cap on thinking alone after 15m40s and returned nothing ($1.28). `croemer` c49883198 / `simonw` c49883294 treat the missing self-limit as a bug. Same failure on Opus 5.5 (`narmiouh` c49813778 under 9.1). This is a stop reason (`max_tokens` with only a thinking block) the loop has to treat as a failed turn, not an empty answer.
- `Leary` c49882300, `manojlds` c49882598, `subscribed` c49883146/c49890102, `shmel` c49896819: the Terminal-Bench gap between Opus and Sonnet partly reflects which model's requests tripped the safeguard and fell back; whether the fallback counts is a scoring policy (the Opus 5.5 post counts it as failure for AutomationBench; AA reports through it). `verdverm` c49884095 left Anthropic over this.
- `square_usual` c49882273, `Jcampuzano2` c49882280, `jtrn` c49882685: above high effort, Opus at low/medium is about the same price and better; reading the system card (unverified, from the comment): prompt-injection success 3.0% for Sonnet 5.5 vs 19.5% for Sonnet 5 and 54.6% for Opus 5.5 in red-team testing, 0.02% benign refusals vs 0.59%, fewer false "I'm done" claims than Sonnet 5 but more than Opus 5.5, thinking "the hardest to read of any model tested". These are `jtrn`'s numbers and I could not open the card.
- `dom96` c49882880: his adversarial-language benchmark fell from 17.8% (Sonnet 5) to 7.4% (Sonnet 5.5) because the new model "returns to ask the user questions whether to keep going" instead of continuing; that is the early-stop behaviour the Haiku prompting page documents and the Opus page tells unattended loops to counter.
- `chrismustcode` c49882670 / `manquer` c49883351 / `esafak` c49884598: model switches bust the prompt cache (no KV sharing across model families); subagents save the parent's compaction. `afro88` c49882728, `hgo` c49821312 (under 9.1): Opus orchestrating, Sonnet implementing; `hgo` reports Opus 5.5 aborted Sonnet subagents mid-run and re-planned with an Opus agent because Sonnet "take shortcuts".
- `klardotsh` c49883966: replaced an agent CI-polling loop with a deterministic CLI polling GitLab and exiting on state change, then a skill that calls it; `solatic` c49888228 replies Claude Code already writes a short polling script itself. A measurable arm: scripted wait vs model-driven wait, on the bill.
- `pavo-etc` c49887870: $ per message from his own harness (pi over xmpp): DeepSeek v4-flash $0.0241 (4,610 messages), v4.1-flash $0.0576 (1,079), after the 28 Sep billing change $0.0365 (69), GPT-6 Luna $0.0329 (88); he suspects long thinking and OpenRouter cache-price variance. Per-message dollars on one harness across models, small n.
**Links followed:** anthropic.com/claude-sonnet-5-5, artificialanalysis.ai/articles/claude-sonnet-5-5, platform docs effort / mid-conversation-system-messages (see 9.1). support.claude.com 14604842 (CVP article): says nothing on fallback mechanics; says CVP "requires data retention" and may be "narrowed or withdrawn". Sonnet system card PDF: too large. code.claude.com channels docs (not opened; wake-on-event for a running session, relevant only as a hint that Claude Code now has push events).
**→ Lain:** The harness-fixed comparisons in this thread are the useful ones, and all three show the same thing: the unit that survives is cost at matched success per effort level, not a model ranking. Terminal-Bench 70.6% vs 64% on the same model, and Sonnet max below Sonnet xhigh because a harness skill timed out, are concrete instances of item 40 and of the "scaffold range exceeds model spread" paper (`2609.17394`, already in the prior list). Lain's own seam: `Bench::Compare` should print, per arm, the share of turns served by a different model than requested (`Assembled#model` is already parsed at `lib/lain/provider/anthropic/stream_assembler.rb` line 27, `anthropic_wire.rb` line 76), and mark an arm "contaminated by fallback" above a threshold; AA's 0.1% for Sonnet 5.5 is the baseline order of magnitude, and `Retr0id`/`mintik` show 100% of a session for cyber-adjacent work. Also: a text-only early stop on `end_turn` (dom96, Anthropic's own unattended-run advice) is a grader-visible failure class that a "turns to success" metric would hide; item 57 (count turns) already points here. `max` effort ending at `max_tokens` with only thinking should become an explicit machine outcome (`lib/lain/stop_reason.rb` already has `MAX_TOKENS` and `TRUNCATED`; the gap is a reply that is `max_tokens` AND has no text/tool block).
**→ ROADMAP:** `### M3c: the bench` for the fallback-contamination counter (new sub-item, extends items 42 and 58); `## Near-term sequence` for the PriceBook rows. Subagent effort inheritance (Claude Code subagents inherit parent thinking level) is "none" for Lain (Lain owns the loop) but is a datum for the orchestration track: effort should be a per-spawn parameter, check `lib/lain/tools/subagent.rb` before claiming it already is.

### 9.3 Claude Haiku 5.5 — id=49996437 (1049pts, 487c)
**Article:** (WebFetch of anthropic.com/claude-haiku-5-5 and the Haiku docs.) Model id `claude-haiku-5-5`. Price per MTok for prompts up to 100k / over 100k: input $0.10 / $0.50, output $0.50 / $2.50, cache read $0.01 / $0.05, cache write $0.125 / $0.625; Haiku 4.5 was $1/$5/$0.10/$1.25. The post says 90% of Haiku 4.5 requests were under 100k. First Haiku with effort (five levels, default `medium`), adaptive thinking on by default, 1M context (was 200k) and 128k output, minimum cacheable prompt 512 tokens (was 4,096), no server-side fallback, no Priority Tier. Same tokenizer as Claude 4.7+, "approximately 30% more tokens" than Haiku 4.5, so $ per token is not comparable with Haiku 4.5 or other labs (`agentdev001` c50007256, unverified). Terminal-Bench 4.0 39.2% (Sonnet 5.5 70.6%), OSWorld 2.1 72.4%, FrontierCode 46.4%. Recommended for "compaction, summarization, classification, and subagents". The Haiku prompting page documents behaviours a loop must handle: at `xhigh` the model sometimes writes its whole answer in thinking and ends the turn with no visible text ("check each response for an empty reply"); at `low` it stops early or skips verification in long agent prompts (`low` to `medium` roughly halved early stopping and more than doubled output tokens per attempt, Anthropic's testing); a user message delivered inside a `tool_result` block, or as a system message right after a tool result, may be ignored because the model resists injection through tool results, so mid-turn user input must be a text block after the last `tool_result` in the same user message; with thinking off plus structured output it can skip a needed tool call; `max_tokens` sized for Haiku 4.5 can cut off a reply; changing top-level `effort` restarts the cache; thinking blocks from other accounts are silently dropped; a request that sends thinking blocks back after `system`, `tools` or earlier messages changed returns 400 (default on accounts created 2026-08-31 or later).
**Comments:** all unverified.
- `minimaxir` c49996502, `TomGarden` c49996629, `Tiberium` c49996710: the 5x step at 100k, a tokenizer that makes Claude's 100k about 60 to 65k GPT tokens, Luna's own threshold at 272k with 2x/1.5x. `jeremyjh` c49999448: most Haiku use is explorer/task subagents under 100k, and rewind in his harness (oh-my-pi) "saves a TON of context". `mkotlikov` c49998519: at higher effort the cost per task exceeds Sonnet 5.5's and the cheap tier never applies.
- `RussianCow` c49999175/c49999357: AA cost per task for Haiku about 3x Luna at every effort; Haiku at max costs about the same as Sol on medium with lower intelligence; value is speed (OpenRouter 93 tok/s, AA at least 137). `Topfi` c49997193: 131 tok/s P50 on OpenRouter. `declanjackson` c49997533: AA reports 162k output tokens per task at max, over double GLM-5.3 Flash. `nharada` c49999411: AAI index Haiku 5.5 43 at $0.10/$0.50 vs MiMo 2.6 Pro 46 at $0.43/$0.87 and Flash 38 at $0.10/$0.28.
- `JMKH42` c50009970: on a trivial counting prompt, low effort "always replied 0", medium was correct (n small, anecdotal), consistent with the low-effort early-stop guidance.
- `thepasch` c49996942 and `cjav_dev`/`luketaylor` (Anthropic staff, c49999765, c49999702): a help-center page was rewritten to say Max/Team plans get monthly API credits covering the Agent SDK, `claude -p` and third-party apps, then restated: `claude -p` and the Agent SDK remain on subscription limits. Policy churn on harness access, not a harness mechanism; noted because a bench that drives Claude through `claude -p` depends on it.
- `alexchamberlain` c49997633, `serf` c49999749: 100k as a subagent budget is fine; people who start every session at 60k of context hurt every result (`serf`, unverified).
**Links followed:** anthropic.com/claude-haiku-5-5, platform.claude.com prompting-claude-haiku-5-5, haiku-5-5/migration-guide, haiku-5-5/whats-new-haiku-5-5 (all read). Not opened: openrouter and AA comparison pages (dynamic), jonclegg pacman-bakeoff, epoch.ai plunging-price-of-thought (cost per capability level falling about 47% per quarter, `istjohn` c49996828, unverified).
**→ Lain:** (a) `lib/lain/context_window.rb` line 63 pins `"haiku" => 200_000`; Haiku 5.5 is 1M (docs, article-sourced). Because the file's stated requirement is "never over-estimate" the bug here is in the safe direction (compaction fires early), so it is a correctness-of-comparison problem, not a crash: a Haiku 5.5 arm would compact at 200k while Sonnet/Opus arms run to 1M. The spec table that tripwires this is named in that file's header (`context_window_spec.rb`). (b) `lib/lain/price_book.rb` line 44 prices all haiku at $1/$5, 10x too high for Haiku 5.5 under 100k and wrong in shape above it; it needs a per-prompt-size tier or an explicit refusal. (c) Haiku 5.5 as the compaction/summarizer model is the vendor's own recommendation: Lain's compaction arms (`lib/lain/compaction/`) could take a summarizer-model axis, but two docs facts constrain it: server-side compaction on 5.5-family models drops thinking blocks before the compaction block, and a cross-model summarizer cannot read the main model's thinking blocks (Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 blocks are not readable by Haiku 5.5, API drops them silently). (d) the mid-turn-user-input rule (never inside `tool_result`) is a Context-render rule Lain can pin with a spec; check the Intake path that lands a prompt while a tool is running before assuming it already does this.
**→ ROADMAP:** `## Near-term sequence` (PriceBook/ContextWindow rows for the 5.5 family) and `### M5: orchestration, memory, code mode`: a "cheap-tier subagent and summarizer" arm. No new item for the subscription-credit churn.

## 9B. Model launches: GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, and the open-weight releases  (SCOPE: harness-evaluation, optimization)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** The two OpenAI threads (about 1,800 comments) are mostly price and vibes, but they carry one strong harness-evaluation case: GPT-6 Sol was replaced by GPT-6.1 Sol after 7 days, and commenters report that the "same" Sol id and Codex got worse then better, with a daily canary (marginlab) already publishing the numbers. They also show that effort level, context size (258k default vs 1M at 2x price), and compaction are the real user-facing harness knobs in Codex, and that cost per task (not per token) flips rankings (Luna 6 is 50% cheaper per token but several users measure more tokens per task, net flat). Open-weight side: Kolibri (78B-A3.46B, Apache 2.0, vendor tau2 numbers) and Beam (501B-A23B, weights promised later in October) are not practical for an ollama arm; Muse Spark Open (816B-A42B) is too big. The practical local-arm names come from comment mentions: Qwen3.8 27B dense, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (n-gram/SSD design, 16 GB VRAM claim), Qwen3.6 35B-A3B (tool-use mistakes reported), Step 3.5/3.7 Flash (128 GB unified). Extends items 58 and 59 of the prior file.

### 9.20 GPT-6 Sol and Luna — id=49805509 (1779pts, 835c)
**Article:** openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-sol-and-luna/ returned HTTP 403 to WebFetch, so article claims below are only as quoted by commenters (unverified). Prices quoted in comments: Luna $0.10 in / $0.50 out per M, cached read $0.20/M for Sol (Readerium c49805763); batch is 50% off (mfiguiere c49805710). A quoted blog line: Luna at max effort scores 66.6%, comparable to Opus 5 and Fable 5 at medium (tabarnacle c49809473). A quoted claim of "code deception 10% at 5.6 to 1.3% for 6.0" (hamburglar1 c49806037). Artificial Analysis (via scrlk c49805991): in OpenAI's Codex harness GPT-6 Sol (max) scores 57 on the Coding Agent Index (+2 vs 5.6 Sol) at $2.99 per task (~50% less), Terminal-Bench 4.0 43% vs 37%; Luna (max) 41, down 2.
**Comments (all unverified):**
- `rbranson` c49806361: Astra planner, Sol reviewer and runner of E2E tests, Luna subagent coder; "about 20% more usage efficient and 20% faster"; works in Codex "with a single-line AGENTS.md instruction". Model-heterogeneous team, same shape as item 53.
- `jeremyjh` c49808527 and c49822480: oh-my-pi roles: Sol high orchestrator, Sol max planner/reviewer, Luna max coder. OMP "rewinds": orchestrator rolls back to an earlier turn with a summary of findings, a checkpoint/rewind setting under Tools. Luna "so much cheaper that even 30K overhead in context is still less costly". Later (c49901784) says rewind stretches the 258k window.
- `jeffnash` c49805972 and `noname120` c49806060: Codex context is ~252k; a config.toml override gives 1M; past the default the excess is billed 2x (`AmazingTurtle` c49899292, `rgbrenner` c49806470); "Codex's compaction is very good but happens so frequently" that Astra loses the plot on long tasks.
- `jorl17` c49811754, `user43928` c49807666: Astra "constantly compacting", ~256k vs 1M on Claude. `Gareth321` c49905613: "2-3 turns before it compacts". One report (c49806 area, line 944 of digest): compaction fired after an instruction was given and before it was acted on, the instruction vanished and the agent reported the task done. This is a concrete compaction-ordering failure worth a test.
- `chrisweekly` c49807573: autocompaction mid-task is lossy at the worst time; compaction at phase boundaries is fine (`slopinthebag` c49812211 agrees). `elcritch` c49813535: Sol is good enough at keeping salient details that he stopped writing planning docs.
- `athrowaway3z` c49808911 and `glub` c49809048: swapping a 200k Fable context into Astra dropped to ~150k tokens; glub says the 50k gap was encrypted reasoning tokens unreadable to the other model. Evidence that a cross-provider mid-session swap changes the context, relevant to item 42.
- `jumploops`/`Alifatisk` c49809220: Codex shipped an experimental context-management mode with Astra; unsure if it applies to Sol/Luna.
- `NickHoff` c49806183, `thatguymike` c49809520, `superfrank` c49807836: Codex has 2 axes (model x effort: light, medium, high, xhigh, max/ultra). "5.6 was the first release where reasoning level really mattered and was finicky": medium under-engineered, high over-engineered. `Cu3PO42` c49806449: in these pelican grids low and max are far apart; `altcognito` c49806493 gives line counts (Astra 105 lines low, 250 max; Luna 90 low, 150 xhigh; time 11s low, 1:33 xhigh for Luna, 33s to 4 min for Astra).
- `pazimzadeh` (6.1 thread) and `heaney-555` c49901963: higher effort sometimes scores lower (6.1 Sol high 75.2% vs xhigh 71.9% on DeepSWE); explanations: lost context, or out-of-scope work penalised. Supports an effort sweep that reports non-monotonic curves.
- `pjankiewicz` c49814239: 36 agent+tool scenarios, 30/36 on Luna 6 vs 33/36 on 5.6 Luna, tuned for 5.6; keeps 5.6. `anelson` c49856057: on a cybersecurity eval 6-Luna uses more tokens at the same effort and is slower, wiping out the 50% saving. `Mazer23` c49824555 (agentic CAD harness): lower price offset by more token use, results improved. `sunaookami` c49899034: "gpt-6-luna leaks tool calls and markers in the output like crazy", gpt-5.6-terra fine. Tool-call hygiene regression under a new id.
- `apitman` c49806451: fixed an OpenAI proxy bug that "completely broke prompt caching"; OpenAI shipped a prompt-caching dashboard (platform.openai.com/usage prompt-caching). `najmbajwa123` (6.1 thread): the API reports `cached_tokens` under input_token_details on every call.
- `sva_` c49806647 and `albert_e` c49812119: when the cache expires, switch to a cheap model to write a handoff note, then resume on the strong model; harness should show a cache-TTL timer. `cruffle_duffle` c49809080: switching models mid-session costs the cache.
- `glub` c49807062, `nl` c49809816: Anthropic's third-party-harness rules were originally about cache-friendly breakpoints; OpenAI lets subscriptions run in other harnesses (pi, OMP, Hermes), so a Codex-subscription arm of a bench is permitted today (unverified).
- `thatguymike`, `lionkor` c49812909: subagents often burn more tokens for the same quality; value is a very cheap subagent for bulk work or review. `josephg`/`HDThoreaun` (6.1 thread): reviewers in subagents work better because they lack the author's reasoning.
**Links followed:** marginlab.ai/trackers/codex/ (see 9.21). openai.com article 403. platform/community Codex context-management thread (see 9.21). matheusmoreira.com code-review article (see below). partforge.ai/blog/2026-09-23-new-model-day: 26 hard CAD tasks x 3 attempts, scored on geometry, process and two reviewer models plus a human ranking; GPT-6 Sol reads ~50% more and takes extra steps, cost per hard task 25.7c (5.6 Sol) vs 25.2c (6 Sol), 92% clean build rate held; Gemini, unchanged, rose 16% in cost between runs because the harness changed. Source: the article. It is a clean example of harness change moving an unchanged model's numbers.
matheusmoreira.com/articles/code-reviewing-lone-lisp-with-sol-and-fable (c49809760, c49809843): author-run, not controlled. 12 of 14 critic lenses x 21 subsystems = 252 cells per model; 538 HIGH/CRITICAL findings deduplicated to 153 unique defects; recall against 106 known issues: Fable 89, Sol 85, 77 shared; McNemar p=0.503 (not significant); cost about $523 Sol vs $1,758 Fable API-equivalent; cyber-policy lockouts: Sol blocked 20 of 21 first attempts on the robustness lens, Fable completed 14 of 21 (Fable downgrades to Opus counted as blocks). Source: the article. Useful because it counts refusals as a harness outcome.
**→ Lain:** (1) Reasoning effort and context cap are provider-level knobs Lain cannot sweep for OpenAI because there is no OpenAI-compatible provider: `lib/lain/provider/http/providers/` holds only `anthropic` (verified) and `lib/lain/provider/` has `anthropic*` and `ollama*` (verified). The comments say the knobs that matter (effort, 258k vs 1M at 2x, compaction trigger, rewinds) are exactly the ones Codex exposes. (2) Item 56 (cache-thrash meter) gets a new data point: `cached_tokens` is a per-call API field and a proxy bug zeroed caching with no error; Lain already parses cached tokens (`lib/lain/provider/http/tokens.rb:35`, verified), so a "cache hit rate fell to 0" alarm is cheap. (3) The "instruction vanished across a compaction" report is a testable invariant for `Compact`: the last user turn must survive and the next request must contain it.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends items 56 and 58; Context table row in ROADMAP "The organizing idea: one seam, many swept axes" (line 46, "prune / compact / recall placement ... cache-aware compaction") gains a trigger-threshold axis (258k vs 1M) and a "compact mid-turn vs at phase boundary" arm. New item: see candidate 3.

### 9.21 GPT 6.1 Sol (replaced 6 Sol after 7 days) — id=49896586 (1067pts, 949c)
**Article:** openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-1-sol/ returned HTTP 403. From comments: cached input is $0.10/M, 95% below standard input and 50% below GPT-6 Sol's cached price (minimaxir c49896762); "Ultrafast" mode up to 8x token speed (c49896929, c49897190); the Pro $200 plan cut from 20x to 10x of Plus usage and a new $500 plan (engadget, `Aboutplants` c49897140; `christkv` c49907394 confirms his plan was cut). Defaulting to fallback metadata; this can degrade performance" at launch (`sergiotapia` c49897165). Pricing page rows from `tensegrist` c49898166: input:output:cached is 10:50:1 for Astra and Luna but 10:50:0.5 for Sol.
**Comments (unverified):**
- Drift under a fixed id, the main yield. `Hammershaft` c49912878: marginlab tracker shows a large drop when the Codex id switched from 5.6 Sol to 6 Sol on the 23rd; `user43928` c49921132: "it released on that date, the data before is a different model". `spwa4` c49898183/c49898198: "Sol 6 was pretty good the first 4 days, then probably dialed back to a lower effort level... repeating pattern: dial the current models to the idiot setting, then release a new model about a week later"; `nxc18` c49898543 claims to predict releases from sudden agent regressions; `Numerlor` c49806536: Sol and Luna "felt notably worse after a couple of weeks". `varispeed` c49806293: run benchmarks 2 to 4 weeks after release; `anthonyrstevens` replies "how do you know". `phoghed` c49897749: perceived decline despite "evidence of the models staying the same from sites that track it". `j_m_b` c49807916: Astra 3D outputs on launch day vs now. All anecdote; the only quantified thing is marginlab.
- `rspeele` c49897552: same task packet, same repo, same test suite, Astra vs Opus 5.5 on $100 plans: Astra used 215% of a week's budget (2 resets) in 13 hours, Opus 20% in 20 hours; Opus found a bug in the supplied unit test, Astra refused to change the test and stubbed B-splines; fresh same-model reviewers preferred their own model's repo (same-model review bias). Matches the prior file's judge-hygiene items (44).
- `rapind` c49899103 and c49900133, `taylorfinley` c49899282, `faitswulff` c49899647: DeepSeek V4.1 Flash via pi: ~3B input tokens per day at ~98.5% cache for ~$35; one user 1.8B tokens in a week, 99.2% cache hits, $18.13 direct from DeepSeek; OpenRouter switches inference providers mid-session so caching degrades; pin with `provider: { order: [...], allowFallbacks: false }` (`pests` c49899963). A provider-pinning requirement for any cloud arm.
- `sieve` c49809000s, c49809840 `MitziMoto`: MiMo provider caches for about an hour, MS13/DS4F only 1 to 5 minutes; production agents are dominated by cache-read price, need `prompt_cache_key`.
- `jbellis` c49899643: ran 100+ tasks on DeepSeek 4.1 Flash, Sol and others; "a little smarter than Sol and a little more expensive than Luna"; spent $280 doing it; slopcop.com power-ranking.
- `gertlabs` c49910597: 100 "unsaturated" coding/engineering environments; Astra and 6.1 Sol ahead of Opus 5.5 and cheaper. `dom96` c49897468/c49806941: private benchmark, 6.1 Sol matches Astra, Opus 5 beats 5.5 on a "understanding" task. `jjcm`, `XCSme`, `jonclegg pacman-bakeoff`: single-prompt visual bakeoffs, conflicting rankings across benchmarks (BugsJustFindMe c49902254). Treat as evidence that rank order is benchmark-dependent.
- `onlyrealcuzzo` c49899193, `codewithcheese` c49897950, `ngruhn` c49899391: "if you compact every 5 minutes you have a workflow problem", Codex compact limit is configurable; `Benjamin_Dobell` c49902914 runs ~600k on a subscription; `RugnirViking` c49905931: large-context costs more.
- `mholm` c49896932: model routers fail because switching models reruns the prefix; Cursor tried and users turned it off. `SkyBelow` c49897160: models do not know new model names, so a hard-coded list of 3 to 4 models in default context is needed. GitHub's Project HydraFusion (c49896... link) is a router: see below.
**Links followed:**
- marginlab.ai/trackers/codex/: daily eval of Codex CLI with GPT-6 Sol (high) on a contamination-resistant subset of SWE-Bench-Pro, N=50 instances a day, no custom harness ("captures harness changes as well as model changes"), 95% CI, p<0.05 degradation test; GPT-5.6 Sol baseline 83.40% (613/735); GPT-6 Sol baseline "Collecting" since 2026-09-24, today 54%, 7-day 59%, 30-day 79%; the page labels deltas -29.4%/-24.9%/-4.6% "significant" while saying verdicts are paused until a baseline exists. Source: the page (summarised by the fetch tool, so the delta labels need a direct look). 6.1 Sol not listed. This is item 58's canary design running in public, and also shows the pitfall: the 30-day window may mix two model ids.
- community.openai.com thread 1395578 (via `Alifatisk` c49809220): Codex 0.153.4 added an experimental context-management mode: token budget for active context, saved history and notes retrievable later, and a `new_context` operation that starts a fresh window and continues from notes, versus default compaction by summary. Enable with `[features.context_management] experimental_mode = true`. No thresholds stated. Direct vendor evidence for the "notes + fresh window vs repeated summaries" axis.
- learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/speed (c49898027): `/fast` and Ultrafast; billed 2.5x / 8x of Standard on subscriptions, 2x / 6x on credits; config `service_tier = "fast"`. Speed is a billed axis separate from effort.
- github.blog Project HydraFusion (Copilot CLI `/experimental`): router picking Single, Cascade (cheap draft then quality gate then escalate) or Critique (draft, read-only critic from another family, one revision) per request; vs Opus 5 baseline: TerminalBench 2.1 +4.9 points at 67% lower est. cost; DeepSWE -1.5 points, 36% lower; internal CheckpointBench -0.1, 65% lower; all at medium reasoning; routing tuned by beam search on a frozen baseline; two harness failures (Aug 11 to 25) produced invalid runs and were excluded. Source: the post. Offline, vendor-run, pool unnamed.
- artificialanalysis.ai release page and `AnodicElegy` c49898478: per-effort price/intelligence comparison; 6.1 Sol max better and cheaper than Opus 5.5 medium, worse than 5.5 high (comment claim; page not fetched).
**→ Lain:** `lib/lain/provider/anthropic_wire.rb:77` (`model: assembled.model`, line was 76 in the prior file; verified) is where the served model is already read, so item 58's mismatch flag is a one-line comparison. What the new evidence adds: a vendor can retire an id's behaviour in a week, so a results table needs a model-release date column and a "snapshot changed during the run window" refusal, and a canary's baseline must restart when the id changes (marginlab's own 30-day number is contaminated). The rspeele experiment is a ready design for a same-task, two-harness comparison with equal task packet and test suite, plus a cross-review matrix (each model reviews both repos) to measure same-model preference.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends item 58 and 44. Quote for the Provider/model row: "Anthropic vs. OpenAI-compatible vs. **local ollama**" (ROADMAP line 50): the OpenAI-compatible arm is named but absent in `lib/lain/provider/`. New item: see candidates 1 and 2.

**Article:** aleph-alpha.com blog (fetched): 78B total, ~3.46B active, 384 experts with 6 active, English and German only, trained to 256k context (1M with extra flags), Apache 2.0, weights `Aleph-Alpha/Kolibri-1` (FP8 default, separate BF16 repo). Serving needs vLLM plus the `aleph-alpha-inference` plugin with `kolibri1` reasoning and tool-call parsers; FP8 KV cache; sampling temp 1.0, top_p 0.97, top_k 128. No llama.cpp or ollama path stated. **Links followed:** aleph-alpha.com blog (above); tech report at aleph-alpha.com/downloads/tech-report.pdf not opened.

## 9C. Model launches: Gemini 4 Argon and Mistral Large 4  (SCOPE: harness-evaluation, optimization)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** Three launch threads (about 3,400 comments), mostly opinion, but four things are measurable. (1) Cost per task is not price per token: Plotly's fixed-harness exam shows Argon reading about 510K tokens per question against about 183K for GPT-6.1 Sol at the same list price, and ariwilson's pi run shows 24K output tokens per run against 3K. Lain's `Usage`/`Price` already record both sides, so this is a Compare axis, not new plumbing. (2) Harness-imposed context policy is a live complaint: Antigravity (agy) forces compaction at 250K on a model with a 1M window, and Copilot compacts Argon near 400K when set to 1M. A fixed-threshold, un-wiggled compaction is a harness variable that moves results while the model name stays the same. (3) Google's 1M-output "long decode" is documented to run in 64K server-side hops, with `total_input_tokens` summed over hops so it overstates context and must not drive summarization. That is a provider-reported-usage trap for Lain's Provider seam. (4) Mistral Large 4 is a 1T-total / 49-52B-active MoE with a preview API now and weights promised for 2026-10-27 under a custom, modified-MIT license, so it is not a usable local arm: roughly 0.5 to 1TB of weights, and the licence is not open for large corporate users. The practical local-arm material is in the comments (Qwen3.8-Flash-Next engines on Strix Halo and RTX 4080 with measured numbers). Several threads repeat benchmark-critique points already in the 2026-09-22 landscape (benchmaxxing, max-effort-only reporting, marginlab-style drift trackers); only the new angles are written up.

### 9.40 Gemini 4 Argon (blog.google launch) — id=49913571 (1705pts, 1197c)
**Article:** (blog.google, fetched.) Argon is not generally released at launch: "trusted cyber defenders" first, then paid API and Ultra. Introductory $2/M input, $10/M output, cached input 95% off; $4/$20 after the intro period. Claims: DeepSWE v1.1 77.9%, AutomationBench 51.3%, LVBench 91.7%, CWE-bench 68%; output limit raised from 64K to 1M tokens (input window is not stated in the post). Agentic claims are internal and unmeasured by outsiders: C/C++ to Rust migrations up to 800K+ lines (Zircon), libgav1 32K lines of SIMD replaced with a result 2.7x faster than the Rust port, fleet memory work freeing 300+ TiB. Safety claims relevant to Lain's seams: CoT and action monitor that can stop execution; findings withheld from training "so as not to shape reasoning to evade monitoring"; Gray Swan indirect prompt injection "leading" with no score. The linked evaluation PDF (storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/gemini/gemini_4_argon_model_evaluation.pdf) is compressed and unreadable by fetch; it only exposes the benchmark link list, so harness, attempts and effort level for the headline numbers are NOT verified.
**Comments (all unverified):**
- `tobias2014` c49915347 / c50080859: agy forces auto-compaction at a fixed 250K tokens on 3.8-flash although the API model takes 1M; `parasti` c49918627 never saw it; `tobias2014` says older versions did it silently, `/context` shows it. `adastra22` c49925725 turns compaction off in Claude Code. `sigseg1v` c49934546 reports large accuracy and token gains by capping Claude at 200K instead of 1M.
- `dudeinhawaii` c50080973: Argon compacts around 400K in Copilot when set to 1M context, "never seen that with other models". `antupis` c50081998: Copilot CLI uses 1.5x the tokens of opencode on their own benchmark. `beardbandit` c50081350: same model, worse results in the Copilot harness.
- `macNchz` c49915937, `marcus_holmes` c49916591, `rnxrx` c49916922, `mikepurvis` c49917520/c49922217: agent-authored handoff (wrap-up skill, MEMORY.md flush then /compact, tickets filed by the agent) beats built-in compaction. Anecdote only; no measurement.
- `robertbarbe` c49918725: Google reports only Max reasoning level; `alpineman` c49919408 says lower effort gave less over-engineered code. `nonethewiser` c49913956 and `MoreThanMe` c49915386: "#1 in 75% of categories" for every lab is possible only with different benchmark subsets, comparator versions and tool budgets; ask for the shared matrix, versions, dates and settings.

(b) Agent-authored handoff vs summarizer compaction is a clean two-arm context experiment with one claim from several independent commenters and zero measurements, so a measurement is the contribution. (c) `lib/lain/usage.rb` and `lib/lain/price.rb` (verified exist) already carry token and price fields: report tokens-per-solved-task next to price so that "same price per token, 2.8x tokens" is visible in `Compare`.

**→ Lain:** Plotly is a usable external design for the bench: one harness held fixed, exam items with a "catch" (impossible, ambiguous, planted data fault, statistics trap), pass/fail where ignoring the catch fails. That maps onto Lain graders that reward an abstain/flag outcome, and its own caveat (2 of 43 is noise) is the case for `Compare` to carry a paired test and a stated N. The long-decode doc is a Provider-seam trap: if Lain ever wraps this endpoint, `Usage` from a hop-summed field would corrupt budget and compaction triggers; the safe rule is to take context size from prompt plus output plus thought, not from the summed input. Gemini offers implicit caching only, so there is no cache-breakpoint arm on it (Lain's `lib/lain/context/cache_breakpoints.rb`, verified exists, has nothing to place); cache-hit would be observed, not controlled.
**→ ROADMAP:** extends "Context" axis (cache-hit/cache-write grading) with a note that a provider can be implicit-only; new item candidate below (tokens-per-solved-task as a first-class Compare column). The drift arm is already item-level in the prior file (marginlab, section 12); this thread only adds the scrollop tracker list.

## 10. Decision models: the Jev pattern  (SCOPE: optimization, harness-evaluation)

**Batch delta in one paragraph.** The prior run (section 6.7, id=49717558) met TypeSafe "Jev" as a hosted, closed "System One" model. In this window the pattern became a family: at least 12 open or vendor clones shipped in 3 weeks (Laya, Kev, Jeff, Jeeves, Strands Decider 2B, Microsoft-Decision-1, Clef from Cloudflare, plus Ollaya as a runtime and Jevstiller as a distiller), OpenAI announced a Decisions API at DevDay, and the first agent-harness uses with numbers appeared (Jeff-Code: a decision model deciding when a coding agent thinks, pass rate 62.4% vs 62.8%, time 0.68x). Mechanically it is a very old trick (read next-token logits over a closed option set) given a calibrated training recipe, a fixed typed API, and a price ($0.042 per 1M input tokens, output free). The bench-relevant content is not the hype but three things: (1) calibration is a measurable, separable property (Brier, ECE, temperature) that Lain's judge does not currently have; (2) a "disagreement bound" (Jevstiller) is a clean way to certify a cheap local stand-in for an expensive judge; (3) Lain's own Ollama provider cannot yet host the pattern because it exposes no logprobs, though Ollama's `/api/chat` does (verified in the docs). Much of the thread mass is astroturf accusations and "this is just a classifier" arguments; I extracted only mechanism, numbers and links.

**What a decision model is, mechanically** (from simonwillison 49796843, the RLCD post 49829625, Arcturus, and the model cards):
- Input: a `state` (string, strings, or key-values) plus 1..N typed questions: `noul` (yes/no, a float 0..1), `choice` (a distribution over caller-supplied options plus a confidence), `score` (a position on a caller-defined scale). Endpoint shape `POST /v1/systemone`; every clone copies it, and TypeSafe's Python SDK 0.7.1 runs unchanged against Ollaya, Kev, Jeeves and Laya.
- Forward pass: one prefill, no autoregressive generation. Options are rendered into the prompt; the answer is read from the logits at one position. Three read-outs exist: (a) plain next-token logprobs over option labels (A/B/C, or an index after a prefilled `choice_index:`; Arcturus 2026-09-21, GLM-Flash post); (b) a learned "pointer head" that scores the hidden state at the last token of each option against the answer-position state (Strands, Kev, Jeeves, Jeff, ~1M parameters, LoRA rank 16 on a Qwen3.5 base); (c) an encoder with a [MASK] per option (Laya, ModernBERT-large 421M).
- Calibration: a temperature fitted per question type on a held-out split by minimising log loss (Strands) or Brier (RLCD). nishtahir (50037949) shows why: Qwen3-1.7B zero-shot is overconfident, bin 0.9-1.0 holds 809 items at mean confidence 0.9855 but accuracy 0.7009; temperature T=3.797 fixes it (post-scaling bin: 109 items, confidence 0.9333, accuracy 0.9541; author-run, CommonsenseQA 1,221 questions).
- Training signal ("RLCD", per the di-zhang post): multiway preference modelling, a Plackett-Luce / softmax cross-entropy over option utilities, then Brier-based calibration. The post is a derivation, not a result; code at github.com/trotsky1997/jevre.
- Cost shape: only input tokens bill. Latency is prefill-bound: Cloudflare reports P50 38.8 ms (Clef-flash), 209.3 ms (Clef) vs 524.1 ms (Jev); Strands ~115 ms on an RTX 3090; Jeff 22 ms on an RTX PRO 6000 (vendor numbers).
- Known failure modes, all from the docs and comments: the option set must contain the right answer ("it cannot choose what it cannot see", TypeSafe jaggedness docs via `suraj_phanindra` c49803528); number of options matters more than the model (GLM-Flash post); option order bias and leakage between options (`fxwin` c49992094); renaming options to synonyms cost GLM-5.3-Flash about 20 points on boolq; Q4_K_M quantisation flipped ~6% of a thinking-router's decisions (Jeff-Code note).

### 10.1 Jev introduces a new shape of LLM; What Is RLCD — id=49796843 (53pts, 20c), id=49829625 (68pts, 8c)
**Article:** Willison (fetched): state in, typed probabilities out; $0.042/MTok input, output free; the post explicitly says it does not explain how the probabilities are computed, warns of bias and tells readers not to rank job applicants with it. RLCD post (fetched) gives the training formulation above and links code and a checkpoint (`huggingface.co/di-zhang-fdu/jevre`), but no learning rates, data size or loss weights.
**Comments (unverified):** `tipsytoad` c49797803 and `wgd` c49886415: it is a chat template, cached option prefix, constrained to a few tokens; "no architectural magic". `vova_hn2` c49798732: every inference framework supports constrained decoding; odd that no provider exposes it. `sysguest` c49800633: a model that cannot emit a malformed answer also hides when it has gone wonky. `time0ut` c49807004: constrained decoding can push output into low-probability regimes (arXiv 2606.21619 cited, NOT vetted, I did not open it). `simonw` c49822931: "an LLM variant ... the only difference is that the output is a set of scores". `hadlock` c49805765: Laya is 1024 tokens vs Jev ~32k; a Qwen 0.8B router has 260k context.
**Links followed:** simonwillison.net post; di-zhang RLCD blog; trotsky1997/jevre (named, not opened).
**→ Lain:** Gives the vocabulary for the mapping in 10.9. The key stance for a study bench is that calibration is a first-class property to measure, separately from accuracy (`foxladmin` c49879036: NLL, Brier, ECE, temperature fit on a split by document, not paraphrase).
**→ ROADMAP:** none by itself; feeds candidate 1 below.

### 10.2 Will OpenAI eat Jev's lunch (comment tree) — id=49802161 (328pts, 233c)
**Article:** Arcturus Labs (fetched). The mechanism in plain terms: read `true`/`false` logits at one position, normalise, or read A-D label tokens. The proposed extension is a `<prediction>` tag mid-reasoning where the decoder reads the logits, writes the number into the text and keeps decoding. Uses named: stop-reasoning check, screening tool-call arguments, scanning tool output for injection, routing to a bigger model. Addendum, 14 days later: OpenAI announced a limited-preview Decisions API at DevDay 2026 (images from day one). I could not open the OpenAI recap (HTTP 403) or the HF explainer (404); the only evidence for the API is this addendum and `JohnBerryman` c49927933, `rgbrgb` c49897606.
**Comments (unverified):** `verdverm` c49802609: Jev is easy to replicate, third parties matched it within a week. `CharlieDigital` c49802641: a 95%-accurate $0.002 / 50 ms classifier is the right tool for "is this a refund request". `BoorishBears` c49792189 (in 49783999): a per-sample GRPO rubric that varies with each sample is the case BERT classifiers cannot cover. `c7b` c49806511: moat is short-lived. `mijoharas`/`ComputerGuru`: "100% synthetic data" is hard to square with an open-model base.
**Links followed:** arcturus post; its 2025-03-31 "Supercharging LLM Classifications with Logprobs" (named).
**→ Lain:** The "predict inside the reasoning" variant is the one that matters for a harness: the decision is a pure function of the prefix, which suits `Context#render` purity, whereas an external decision model is an Effect-like call. Two distinct seams.
**→ ROADMAP:** none.

### 10.3 Open-weight clones, measured — ids 49783999 (Kev), 49883844 (Jeff), 49891290 (Jeeves), 49987076 (Strands), 50024913 (Microsoft), 49923692 (Clef), 49857656 (GLM-Flash), 50037949 (Build your own)
**Article:** All fetched. Summary table, all author- or vendor-reported:

| Model | Base | Head / method | Reported | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| Jeeves (PostHog, 242pts) | Qwen3.5-9B | LoRA + pointer head; **reasons only when calibrated confidence < 0.9**; diffusion drafter | held-out 0.905 vs Kev-9B 0.822, Jev 0.857; reasoned on 39% of items; ~20 ms without thinking, 1.2 s median with (H100 FP8) | torch, CUDA or Apple Silicon 48GB+ |

| Microsoft-Decision-1 (200pts) | Qwen3.5-9B post-trained | rubric grading of "AI responses and agent actions" | best of 36 benchmarks / ~150k blind questions (no figures in the post); decision flips on 1.3% of inputs over 8 perturbation types; $0.042/MTok | Foundry hosted |
| Clef / Clef-flash (Cloudflare, 642pts) | frozen Qwen3.8-27B / Qwen3.5-9B | rank-256 adapters + routing head; vision, 64k ctx | P50 209 ms / 38.8 ms vs Jev 524 ms; beat Jev on 3 of 4 TypeSafe workflow evals; open weights Apache 2.0 | Workers AI; commenter says llama.cpp runs it |
| GLM-5.3-Flash prefill trick (138pts) | no fine-tune | prefill `choice_index:`, read logprobs of index tokens | on par with Jev over 29 datasets (median gap 0.7 pts, not significant); outputs changed up to 3.5% between identical temp-0 runs; EUR 62 vs EUR 16 per 1M decisions; reasoning on costs ~EUR 350 per 1M | vLLM `logprob_token_ids` |

Lessons that are about method, not product: Strands' author (Brooker blog, fetched) says the pointer head was the biggest single gain over a 53k-parameter slot head that learned positional bias; he admits seeing JevBench examples (contamination) and that unseen-task generalisation is modest; "templated synthesis" and easy-problem distillation did not help. Jeeves says reasoning-based answers are overconfident and need a larger temperature, so calibration must be fitted per mode. GLM-Flash: "number of options affects accuracy more than the choice between GLM and Jev".
**Comments (unverified):** `AgentMasterRace` c49884862: Jeff 70% vs Jev 94% on their classification. `woadwarrior01` c49989482: Intern-Decision family (0.8B/2B/4B) has the same base and pointer head. `Topfi` c50028142: Microsoft-Decision-1 was 0.72x cost but slower (283 vs 369 ms p50) than Jev on a small suite. `agrippanux` c49929048: Jev first pass, then an Ollama model on Workers AI only when unsure for moderation; Clef was 2-3x slower and caught less hate speech. `hrpnk` c49989024: the PEFT/LoRA to GGUF conversion for pointer-head models is left to the user and failed on a `convert_lora_to_gguf.py` dry run; a reply says the pointer head needs custom layers, so a plain LoRA conversion is not enough. `mijoharas` c49992865 asked for an Ollama or llama.cpp route and got Ollaya (10.4).
**Links followed:** all eight model pages; github.com/jaredpalmer/kev, PostHog/jeeves, firelex/jeff, nishtahir/build-your-own-jev (named), brooker.co.za/blog/2026/09/28/engineering-system-one.
**→ Lain:** The family is a ready-made arm axis for a "judge/triage/router" sweep: same question, five stand-ins, one scored corpus. The inference stack is the practical blocker (see 10.4 and 10.9).
**→ ROADMAP:** candidate 1 and 2.

### 10.4 Ollaya, and does anything run under Ollama? — id=49848269 (618pts, 145c)
**Article:** ollaya.dev (fetched). Apache-2.0 single binary "ollaya run winnow:e4b", ONNX Runtime on CPU/NVIDIA, or llama.cpp for some GGUFs; endpoints `/v1/systemone`, `/v1/decisions`, `/v1/models` on 127.0.0.1; 21 model families (laya, kev, decider, winnow, nimble, qwen3guard...). Claims winnow:e4b 89 ms and 0.722 accuracy on an RTX 4090 vs hosted Jev 0.738 at 236-276 ms; and on the same Nimble weights calibration error 0.022 vs 0.122 on "Ollama 0.35" (but Ollama faster, 210 vs 310 ms). Self-reported.
**Answer to "does it run under Ollama":** no model in this batch ships an Ollama Modelfile. Ollaya is a separate server that imitates Ollama's UX (page says not affiliated). Jeff and Clef go through llama.cpp (Jeff documents `--lora` and 20 ms adapter swap in llama-server); `NakliTechie/llamacpp-jev` (c49858243, fetched) wraps an **unmodified `llama-server`** (`/apply-template`, `n_probs`, GBNF) and serves `/v1/systemone` for any GGUF, but probabilities are "raw label softmax, not calibrated", Qwen3.5 0.8B and 2B "collapse on 64-way choices", and 526 ms median for 4 image questions on an M4 Pro. So the prefill trick works on any local engine that returns logprobs; the learned-head models need custom code and do not convert. Ollama's own `/api/chat` has `logprobs` and `top_logprobs` request fields and a `logprobs` response array (verified in docs.ollama.com/api/chat), so the GLM-style recipe (no fine-tune, uncalibrated, then fit a temperature) is runnable on Lain's existing Ollama backend with one provider change.
**Comments (unverified):** `emmettbt` c48848421 / `george_max` c49848485: Ollama can add this any time. `nickstinemates` c49850124: Laya on a GTX 970 4GB replacing Jev API use. `networked` c49932279: used Ollaya for local testing of Clef Flash. `hadlock` c49805765 as above.
**Links followed:** ollaya.dev; llamacpp-jev; docs.ollama.com/api/chat.
**→ Lain:** `lib/lain/provider/ollama/` has `encoding.rb`, `decoding.rb`, `stream_assembler.rb` and no logprobs handling (verified: grep for `logprobs` across `lib/lain/provider/ollama*` returns nothing). So the first step is a request field and a response field, plus a Mock counterpart.
**→ ROADMAP:** candidate 2.

### 10.5 Jevstiller: distill a hosted judge into a local model with a bound — id=49891769 (67pts, 16c)
**Article:** jevstiller.pages.dev (fetched). A proxy trains a frozen bge-small embedding plus multinomial logistic head on Jev's full probability vectors, retrains every 2,000 answers, shadow-tests before promotion, and answers locally (~15 ms CPU, vs ~300 ms) only when a kNN out-of-distribution score and a confidence threshold allow. The guarantee: pick a target (98%) so the local answer equals what Jev would have returned on at least that share; per task agreement = 1 - c*e (coverage times local disagreement rate), threshold chosen by fixed-sequence testing with an exact Clopper-Pearson bound, fitted at 85% of the budget then rechecked. On 5 public tasks x 20 random splits, a point-estimate threshold broke the 2% budget in 6 to 12 splits per task; the bound did it in 0 to 1, costing 4 to 8 points of coverage. A permanent 2% audit slice goes to the teacher; on a breach everything falls back and retrains (24-hour soak: a simulated teacher change at hour 12 dropped local share from 90% to 9%, recovered in 49 minutes). Limits stated: measures agreement with the teacher, not truth; assumes calibration data is a random sample of production.
**Comments (unverified):** `tgluck` c49891791 (author) summary of the above. `mjyoke1111` c49791073 (in 49783999): a logistic head has no "none of the above"; log confidence margins, set an abstain threshold, route the tail to a bigger model.
**→ Lain:** This is the cleanest published recipe for a *measured* cheap stand-in for an expensive grader or judge, and it is a bench object: coverage-vs-disagreement curves are a metric a Lain experiment can report. The stationary-sample assumption is exactly what breaks when an agent's call distribution shifts mid-session, which is the approval judge's situation.
**→ ROADMAP:** candidate 3.

### 10.6 Decision models inside coding-agent harnesses — ids 49837537, 49888632, comments in 49783999 and 49883844
**Jeff-Code (firelex c49976177; jeffhub.ai/notes/jeff-v1-3, fetched, authors disclose involvement):** a Pi fork (MIT) where two Jeff v1.3 LoRA adapters sit around each Qwen3.8-27B turn: one runs read-only information gathering before the turn (read, list, search, check tools; test/build/install need run approval; "writing and editing files always stay with Qwen"), one decides whether the next turn needs thinking (threshold 0.6; about 27% of turns needed none). Paired by task over 1,242 tasks: pass rate 62.4% vs 62.8% (-0.2, 95% interval -2.6 to +2.1); time per task 0.68x (geometric mean, 0.64-0.72) but total time only 0.86x because ~5% of tasks run 30+ minutes longer; thinking off everywhere cost 7.6 points. Per benchmark time ratio 0.63x SWE-bench Verified to 0.96x Terminal-Bench 2.0. Thinking capped at 8,000 tokens with a loop guard and a runaway cut-off. Q4_K_M flips ~6% of thinking-router decisions vs full precision; use Q8_0.
**Jev-pilot (49837537, fetched):** one ~0.5 s Jev call per prompt via Claude Code function hooks (2.1.278+, early access) picks effort (5 levels), subagent model, strategy and one skill; sends the prompt, last 4 messages capped at 2,000 chars, no tool I/O. Asymmetric thresholds: raise effort at confidence 0.3, lower at 0.6, `xhigh` needs 0.6, subagent downgrade 0.6, `graph` 0.8; 2 failed tool calls raise effort once; 1,500 ms timeout falls back to stock behaviour. Author-run bench, 8 tasks, both pass all 8, 13% cheaper ($1.92 vs $2.20), one hard bug where it spent more; 16 of 40 real subagent briefs moved to Sonnet. Small and self-run, say so.
**Jev Decision Layer (49888632, fetched):** MCP server with 20 tools; local gate returns `act`, `verify` or `ignore`; fails closed to `verify`; no shipped recipe can return `act`. The title says "save frontier tokens" but the README says the project "has not measured savings in your workflow"; no caching of decisions is described.
**Strands intervention handler (Strands blog, fetched):** two yes/no questions about a pending tool call, handler returns Proceed, Deny, Confirm or Guide.
**Comments (unverified):** `IronWolve` c49794767: proxy where the decision model narrows the tool list before the frontier call, "about 60% less tool calls"; `yt1998` c49800034 asks for completion rates and cites Gear (a harness change took pass rate 36% to 40% with 14 tasks improving and 10 regressing). `NitpickLawyer` c49723571 (prior run) had suggested the overseer-after-each-step idea. `agrippanux` as in 10.3 is the tiered "decision model first, LLM when unsure" pattern.
**Links followed:** jeffhub note; jev-pilot; jev-decision-layer; rsi-gear/gear (opened: "RSI infrastructure for refining agent harnesses", inner task loop plus outer meta-agent; its page does not contain the 36% to 40% experiment, I could not verify it).
**→ Lain:** (a) Jeff-Code is the first reported number for "a small model decides when the big one thinks": equal pass rate, lower wall time, with a *paired* design and an interval, which is the shape Lain's bench reports. It is a router arm for the existing "adaptive router" row. (b) Jev-pilot's asymmetric raise/lower thresholds with a timeout fallback is a worked example of fail-open routing; the approval judge fails *closed* (defer or fault goes to the asking rung, verified in `ARCHITECTURE.md` lines 661-666), so the two must not share a fallback policy. (c) The tool-narrowing proxy is a context-rendering treatment (`Toolset` filtering per call), and breaks the prompt-cache prefix on every change (CLAUDE.md: "a scope never changes the toolset, so a flip cannot move the tool block a prompt cache keys on"), a cost nobody in these threads measured.
**→ ROADMAP:** candidate 2 and 4.

### 10.7 Jev alternatives are an old idea: classifier baselines: comments in 49796843, 49802161, 49783999
**Comments (unverified):** `Onavo` c49803732 and `infamia` c49802860: GLiNER, BERT zero-shot, spaCy and classic ML may beat an LLM on bounded labels; `constantlm` c49793062: fine-tuned ModernBERT from ~30% zero-shot to 98.2%, ~150 ms on a laptop; `nico` c49789123 and `janalsncm` c49898542: always include a trained embedding + logistic baseline, and do not use accuracy for spam. `davidweatherall` c49802683 (noun-phrase extraction): spaCy splits "the biggest beer in the world" wrongly, which is why he used Jev. `lgas` c49852273 (jevplay) and `cjonas` c49853893: Laya falls apart on query variations vs Jev and Decider. `hbrn` c49850386 and `verdverm` c49851780: Laya is among the lowest-scoring Jev-alikes on third-party benchmarks, and there is a dispute over who had the idea first; the Laya card itself says its Jev numbers are "third-party published, never measured here", base checkpoints score near chance zero-shot (0.362 vs 0.461 majority-class), and `action.act_probability` has no usable signal. Benchmarks named: JevBench (github.com/fstandhartinger/jevbench, HF space multimodalart/jev-decision-index, benchmarkheaven.com/jev-models); `janalsncm` c49810832 notes every JevBench question is single-dimensional. S Anand's BANKING77 test (via sgnt.ai) had Jev slightly faster and cheaper than DeepSeek v4.1 Flash and GPT-5.6 Luna but less accurate (77 samples).
**Links followed:** huggingface.co/convaiinnovations/laya; sgnt.ai/p/jev (4 open reproductions: openjev 84.5% agreement vs Jev 88.3% on 102 decisions, ~5x faster than asking for JSON; openjev-sglang MMLU-Pro Jev 83% vs Qwen3.8-27B 60.0%, BoolQ 89% vs 91.56%).
**→ Lain:** Any decision-model arm in a Lain sweep needs a trained-classifier baseline and a prompted-LLM baseline, or the comparison is the thing the commenters are rightly mocking.
**→ ROADMAP:** candidate 1.

### 10.8 Uses outside agents (dropped detail, kept one line)
Demos in 49802160: noun-phrase pick as the user types (`davidweatherall`, 1,000 requests for $0.01), no-space autocorrect (`levmiseri`), meal-list categorisation 30-60 s on DeepSeek V4 Flash vs <1 s (`liquicity` c49803734), prompt-injection yes/no at gpu.studio/jev (`lanyard-textile` c49804711: 95% yes on an obvious injection, one example). `xavortm` c50040366: chunk filtering before the LLM "reduces token usage at least 10x on average" (self-reported). None changes the Lain reading.

### 10.9 Mapping to Lain's four seams (verified against WT)
- **Approval judge (`lib/lain/approval/judge.rb`).** Today a fresh-root `auto_approver` child, `TIMEOUT = 30`, `CONTEXT_MODE = :fresh`, and only a lone `approve|deny|defer` token counts as a verdict (the `VERDICT` regex), anything else is `:defer`, error or timeout is `:fault`. A decision model fits as a *pre-judge* on the two cheapest rungs: a yes/no "does this call write outside the named place?" with a two-sided threshold and an undecided band that falls through to the child. That is what the prior run's Pi-jev-auto-mode did (193-642 ms per call). It would give the judge what it lacks: a calibrated number, so "defer" stops being a free-text hedge. It does not cure the fragmented-attack blind spot (plan item 46 still stands): a stateless per-call classifier has the same TPR=FPR problem.
- **Auto router / `auto` mode.** Same pre-judge idea, plus the "adaptive router" row in ROADMAP (grep: "adaptive router" in the orchestration axis table, line 51). Jeff-Code and Jev-pilot are two reported router arms; both need a control arm (plan item 42's "router/decider sweeps refuse a treatment fork without its control").
- **Shell verdict (`lib/lain/shell/verdict.rb`).** Triage reads argv words of a parsed term; a command the parser cannot parse is not checked word by word under `bypass` (CLAUDE.md). A decision model cannot repair an unparseable command, and should not be on this path: the repo's rule is that the boundary be a place a reader can find. It is a candidate only for the *advisory* "is this command about what the user asked" question, not for the verdict.
- **Compaction trigger (`lib/lain/compaction/need.rb`, `scheduler.rb`).** `Need` asks WHETHER, `Scheduler` WHEN, and its comment explains the cache-warmth reasoning. A calibrated "has a sub-problem just been resolved" is a candidate input to `Need`, as the prior run noted for 2606.23525. The new evidence is only that the Jeeves/Jeff pattern of "answer directly above a calibrated threshold, escalate below" is a drop-in shape for `Scheduler`'s defer-vs-force decision.
